**Summary.** This paper presents a novel Lorentzian Mixture Mean-Field (LMMF) method designed to analyze coupled oscillator networks whose natural frequencies follow arbitrary distributions. By approximating the true distribution as a sum of Lorentzians, it achieves significant dimensionality reduction while maintaining high accuracy compared to existing methods. This technique is successfully applied both to theoretical Kuramoto models and to analyzing complex electrophysiological data from real neurons.

**Why it may be interesting.** The development of a robust mean-field reduction technique applicable to arbitrary disorder distributions is highly valuable. While the system itself is classical/biological, the mathematical machinery (dimensionality reduction via spectral methods applied to complex systems) shares conceptual links with analyzing many-body quantum states or effective Hamiltonians in disordered quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is understanding the complex dynamics of globally coupled phase oscillator systems when the intrinsic frequencies ($\omega_i$) are heterogeneous (arbitrary distributions). The goal is to achieve a low-dimensional mean-field description for such systems.

**Main result.** The authors introduce the Lorentzian Mixture Mean-Field (LMMF) method, which successfully extends dimensionality reduction techniques from simple frequency distributions (like Lorentzian) to arbitrary empirical distributions by approximating them as mixtures of Lorentzians.

**Method.** The core method involves approximating an arbitrary probability distribution $ho(\omega)$ using a finite sum of Lorentzian functions ($ho_M^*(\omega)$). This approximation is then used within the LMMF framework to derive low-dimensional mean-field equations for system observables like average phase coherence $R(t)$.

**Model / system.** The model studied is globally coupled phase oscillators (e.g., Kuramoto models) and, in an application context, Quadratic Integrate-and-Fire (QIF) neural networks. Heterogeneity is introduced via the intrinsic frequency distribution $ho(\omega)$ or the distance-to-threshold $D_i$.

**Key observables.** Average phase coherence dynamics ($R(t)$), bifurcation structure (e.g., Hopf bifurcations, fold bifurcations), and steady-state firing rates.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The heterogeneity parameters defining the Lorentzian mixture components ($\mathbf{u}$ in Eq. 5) and global coupling strength $K$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that an arbitrary distribution can be accurately approximated by a finite sum of Lorentzian distributions ($M$ ensembles). The LMMF derivation itself relies on mean-field approximations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the fitting performance of the LMMF model to empirical data (e.g., Gaussian mixtures for frequency distributions) and compare the resulting dynamics ($R(t)$) against previous theoretical approaches, showing superior accuracy and lower dimensionality.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the general mean-field reduction technique by approximating arbitrary distributions as Lorentzian mixtures. It then applies this to Kuramoto oscillators, comparing LMMF results favorably against prior methods (Skardal's). Finally, it demonstrates the method's utility by applying it to analyze electrophysiological data from cortical neurons.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Understanding the dynamical properties of coupled phase oscillator systems with heterogeneous oscillator frequencies has been a long-standing challenge of complex systems theory. While the seminal work of Ott and Antonsen dramatically improved our theoretical understanding of coupled phase oscillators for a small family of oscillator frequency distributions, we here present a mean-field reduction method for arbitrary frequency distributions. Our method leverages the drastic dimensionality reduction obtained for Lorentzian frequency distributions, and combines it with a data-driven multi-ensemble approach. As such, the method renders the Ott-Antonsen equations directly applicable to empirical distributions of phase oscillator frequencies, often achieving a drastic dimensionality reduction and allowing to study real-world physical and biological systems by means of stability, sensitivity, and bifurcation analyses.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes incompatibility in quantum mechanics using partial Boolean algebras (pBAs) and introduces the 'exclusivity graph' as a structural tool. The main finding is that many incompatible scenarios can be mathematically shown to be equivalent to structures embeddable into classical Boolean algebras, suggesting that mere incompatibility alone does not guarantee non-classicality.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides deep structural insights into the mathematical foundations of quantum contextuality, which is crucial for understanding measurement limitations in any quantum system, including those studied in AMO physics or quantum information theory.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research investigates the structural properties of incompatibility scenarios in quantum mechanics and determines if these scenarios are fundamentally non-classical or can be realized classically.

**Main result.** Every exclusivity graph is proven to be the atom graph of an exclusive partial Boolean algebra (pBA), which can be embedded into a standard Boolean algebra, suggesting that many incompatible scenarios are classically realizable.

**Method.** The authors use the mathematical framework of partial Boolean algebras (pBAs) and introduce the 'exclusivity graph' to precisely characterize incompatibility structures. They develop a unified method to map these abstract structures onto classical games.

**Model / system.** The model is based on quantum observables whose incompatibilities are studied using pBAs, which capture the logical structure of measurement outcomes. The analysis compares this framework against standard Boolean algebras and known contextuality examples like the Peres-Mermin square.

**Key observables.** Incompatibility itself, exclusivity graphs, partial Boolean algebras (pBA), and atom graphs.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The specific case $(1, 2, 2)$ for two incompatible dichotomic observables is used as a primary example.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the logical structure of incompatibility can be captured by pBAs, and it notes limitations regarding the full characterization (necessary/sufficient conditions) for exclusivity graphs.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the partial Boolean algebra $A(1,2,2)$ for two observables, the associated exclusivity graph, and examples like the Peres-Mermin square demonstrating compatibility structures.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the pBA framework to study incompatibility. It introduces the exclusivity graph as a characterization tool, proves that these graphs correspond to specific types of pBAs (epBA), and concludes by showing how these structures can be mapped onto classical games.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Incompatibility constitutes a fundamental aspect of quantum mechanics. However, not every quantum observable non-classical property arises from incompatibility, nor can all quantum scenarios be fully captured by incompatibility alone. Within the framework of partial Boolean algebra (pBA), we research the structural properties of incompatibility scenarios. We introduce a unified method to realize any incompatibility scenario via a classical game, and the construction is extendable to any scenario embeddable into a Boolean algebra. The exclusivity graph offers a precise characterization of incompatibility scenarios. We prove that every exclusivity graph is the atom graph of an exclusive pBA, which is embedded into a Boolean algebra. These results provide a necessary condition for exclusivity graphs and a sufficient condition for atom graphs.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes how different types of nonlinear particle detectors respond when placed across a Rindler firewall. By comparing couplings to local observables like energy density versus momentum, the authors demonstrate that quadratic couplings lead to unphysical divergences at the horizon. This strongly suggests that such non-linear interactions are incompatible with the sharp firewall picture.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant to theoretical physics concerning quantum field theory in curved spacetime, black hole thermodynamics, and the information paradox, touching upon concepts of entanglement structure across horizons.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the response of nonlinear particle detectors coupled to local observables (like energy density or momentum) when crossing a Rindler firewall.

**Main result.** Nonlinear couplings, particularly those involving quadratic momentum terms, lead to unavoidable formal divergences at the firewall, suggesting incompatibility with the standard sharp firewall model. The pathologies are attributed to the discontinuity of correlations across the horizon rather than the detector model itself.

**Method.** The authors employ a distributional framework and perturbative expansion in the coupling strength to calculate detector response functions using two-point correlation functions derived from the Wightman function.

**Model / system.** The system involves Unruh-DeWitt detectors coupled to a quantum scalar field ($\hat{\phi}$) in Minkowski spacetime, specifically analyzing the state mimicking correlations across the Rindler horizon (firewall). Couplings are tested for local observables: energy density ($\hat{T}_{\mu
u}$) and quadratic momentum ($\hat{\pi}^2$).

**Key observables.** Detector response function ($F_0$), Energy Density Correlation Function, Quadratic Momentum Correlation Function.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Coupling strength ($\lambda$), Detector switching function $\chi(t)$, Dimensionality (1+1D vs 3+1D), and the energy gap ($\Omega$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes an initial state $\hat{ho}_0 = |gangle\langle g| \otimes |0angle\langle 0|$ and utilizes normal ordering for stress-energy density renormalization.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare dimensionless response functions $F(\pi)_0$ and $rac{T^2}{2} F(\pi^2)_0$ versus frequency ($\omega$) in 1+1D, and illustrate the propagation of decoupled sectors across the firewall.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops a distributional framework to calculate detector response functions for different couplings. It compares results in 1+1D and 3+1D dimensions, showing proportionality relationships between energy density and momentum coupling responses while highlighting divergences specific to nonlinear terms at the firewall.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate Unruh-DeWitt detectors coupled to composite observables of a quantum scalar field, including quadratic coupling to the field momentum and coupling to the local energy density. We develop a distributional framework for evaluating the corresponding detector response functions and apply it to detectors crossing the Rindler firewall. While we recover the finite response of the derivative-coupling model, we show that quadratic momentum coupling leads to ill-defined products of distributions and unavoidable formal $δ(0)$-type divergences. Since the local energy-density response is closely tied to the quadratic momentum response, our results provide strong evidence that the standard sharp firewall model is incompatible with nonlinear detector couplings to local observables. Our analysis further suggests that these pathologies originate from the discontinuous severing of correlations across the Rindler horizon, rather than from the detector model itself.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates the quantum behavior of magnetic spin textures like skyrmions and hopfion rings using advanced computational techniques. By employing the Cluster Mean-Field Approximation alongside DMRG, the authors overcome dimensionality limitations to accurately calculate quantum fluctuations in these complex topological states. The results highlight where classical micromagnetics breaks down, suggesting necessary extensions to the theoretical framework.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on magnetism, the heavy reliance on DMRG, cluster mean-field theory, and mapping complex Hamiltonians onto solvable 1D/2D effective models makes this highly relevant for developing numerical techniques applicable to other quantum many-body systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study focuses on accurately computing the quantum properties of topologically non-trivial spin textures, such as skyrmions and hopfion rings.

**Main result.** The Cluster Mean-Field Approximation (CMFA) successfully computes quantum fluctuations in large spin textures and accesses metastable states, revealing limitations in purely classical micromagnetic models.

**Method.** The authors combine the CMFA with DMRG simulations by exploiting cylindrical symmetry to reduce the problem to an effective 1D chain solvable via Matrix Product States.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled using a Heisenberg Hamiltonian describing chiral magnets, analyzed in both discrete lattice forms and continuum approximations adapted for cylindrical coordinates.

**Key observables.** Topological indices (Q, H), local variation of magnetization length |m|, quantum fluctuations, and zero-mode dynamics of the Hopfion ring.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis explores intermediate regimes between purely classical and fully quantum spin textures; key parameters include exchange stiffness (A), DMI constant (D), and anisotropy (K).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The CMFA treats inter-cluster interactions at a mean-field level, which is an approximation that may underestimate the true quantum fluctuations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate DMRG/CMFA results for skyrmion profiles in various geometries and show convergence analysis (energy, variance) of 2D DMRG simulations as bond dimension increases.

**Paper structure.** The paper moves from defining the continuum micromagnetic Hamiltonian to adapting it into cylindrical coordinates. It then details the application of CMFA combined with DMRG to simulate quantum fluctuations in skyrmion and hopfion ring structures, concluding with comparisons to classical limits.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the quantum properties of two- and three-dimensional spin textures -- $kπ$-skyrmions and hopfion rings -- within the cluster mean-field approximation (CMFA). By combining the CMFA with a symmetrization procedure, we achieve two key advances: the accurate computation of quantum fluctuations in large spin textures and reliable access to metastable states. These challenges are generally insurmountable using standard methods, which are severely limited by the curse of dimensionality and typically restricted to ground-state properties. Exploiting the cylindrical symmetry of the studied magnetic configurations, we construct one-dimensional chain-like clusters that can be efficiently simulated using the density matrix renormalization group method, while inter-cluster interactions are treated at the mean-field level. The resulting spatial profiles of quantum features such as the local variation of the magnetization length in hopfion rings reveal limitations of the classical micromagnetic model and indicate the necessity of its extension. We demonstrate that the recently proposed regularized micromagnetic equation provides a suitable framework for this purpose.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper investigates Fractional Anomalous Hall Crystals in rhombohedral graphene by calculating their total energy using Monte Carlo simulations. The authors demonstrate that these topologically ordered states are energetically viable candidates compared to simpler phases like Fermi liquids. This work supports a continuum-and-interactions-first approach for realizing fractional quantum anomalous Hall physics in real materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, materials-based pathway (graphene moiré systems) to realize exotic topological states like FAHCs, bridging abstract condensed matter theory with solid-state material physics relevant to quantum phenomena.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to establish Fractional Anomalous Hall Crystals (FAHCs) as energetically competitive candidate states in a realistic physical setting, moving beyond purely theoretical models.

**Main result.** FAHCs were found to be energetically competitive with integer anomalous Hall crystals and Fermi liquids. Their stability is governed by the interplay between interaction energy inherited from a parent Quantum Hall liquid and kinetic energy favoring specific crystal periods.

**Method.** The study employs Monte Carlo simulations and semi-analytic evaluations using variational wavefunctions derived from ideal continuum models, followed by restoring realistic dispersion and screened Coulomb interactions.

**Model / system.** The system studied is rhombohedral pentalayer graphene (R5G) or R5G/hBN heterostructures. The physics involves modeling topological order similar to the fractional quantum Hall effect without an external magnetic field, characterized by spontaneous breaking of continuous translation symmetry.

**Key observables.** Energetic competitiveness relative to IAHCs and FLs; anomalous Hall response (integer vs. fractional); stability dependence on twist angle and displacement field.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Filling fraction ($
u$), twist angle, displacement field ($u_D$), and the strength of the periodic potential ($U_0$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on using ideal continuum models to generate variational wavefunctions before incorporating physical dispersion and interactions; approximations are used in constructing the AHC wavefunctions.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically shows how a weak periodic potential can pin an FAHC at fractional filling ($
u < 1$) below competing states. Other figures compare energy calculations (MC vs. analytical) for kinetic and interaction energies across various parameters.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the theoretical framework using ideal continuum models to define AHC wavefunctions, then performing detailed energy evaluations via Monte Carlo simulations incorporating realistic interactions, and finally mapping out the stability phase diagram based on these energetic comparisons.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Fractional anomalous Hall crystals (FAHCs) replicate the topological order of the fractional quantum Hall effect in the continuum without requiring any external magnetic field. They spontaneously break continuous translation symmetry like a Wigner crystal, but are distinguished by each unit cell holding a fixed fractional number of electrons. Until now, these states have been confined to theoretical speculation or engineered models, leaving open the question of whether they can plausibly emerge in actual physical systems. Here, we establish them as energetically competitive candidate states in a realistic material setting. We study rhombohedral pentalayer graphene (R5G) with variational wavefunctions that are exact zero modes of a recently proposed ideal model of R5G. We evaluate their energies using Monte Carlo, after reinstating realistic dispersion and screened Coulomb interactions. We find FAHCs to be energetically competitive with integer anomalous Hall crystals and Fermi liquids, and their stability follows a simple principle. Each crystal maps onto a parent quantum Hall liquid that fixes its interaction energy, while the kinetic energy favors crystal periods that match the finite-momentum minimum of R5G's Mexican-hat dispersion. A weak periodic potential can then selectively lower and pin the commensurate fractional crystals. This picture predicts how the integer and fractional quantum anomalous Hall stability windows evolve with twist angle and displacement field, which we compare to recent experiments. These results support a continuum-and-interactions-first route to fractional anomalous Hall states in rhombohedral graphene.

</details>

**Authors:** J. V. M. Steimetz, M. Campisi, M. V. S. Bonança

**Summary.** The paper extends fluctuation theorems to thermally isolated quantum systems by deriving detailed and integral forms. It establishes quantitative links between non-equilibrium work fluctuations and thermodynamic quantities like excess work and relative entropy. This provides a rigorous, quantum mechanical framework for understanding the minimal energy cost associated with driving quantum processes.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides rigorous quantum generalizations of fundamental theorems like the Second Law for driven systems, which is crucial for understanding energy dissipation and irreversibility in open or isolated quantum dynamics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is deriving detailed and integral fluctuation theorems for thermally isolated, driven quantum systems to establish thermodynamic inequalities stronger than those provided by the Jarzynski equality.

**Main result.** The authors derive relationships connecting the average of work fluctuations to physical quantities like nonadiabaticity (relative entropy) and excess work, establishing a minimal work principle for irreversible processes.

**Method.** The analysis employs quantum fluctuation theorems, utilizing time-reversal symmetry ($\Theta$) and defining stochastic variables based on energy measurements at initial and final states.

**Model / system.** The system is a quantum mechanical entity governed by a time-dependent Hamiltonian $H(\lambda_t)$, evolving from an initial Gibbs state $ho(0) = \Pi_{eta}(\lambda_0)$ under external driving protocols.

**Key observables.** Work ($X$), nonadiabaticity parameter ($A$), excess work ($	ext{W}_{	ext{ex}}$), and the relative entropy $D[\dots]$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature $eta$, time-dependent parameters $\lambda(t)$, and the initial/final Hamiltonian eigenstates.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation of key results relies on highly restrictive conditions (e.g., Eq. 96) which are not generally valid, and assumptions about the nature of entropy production in isolated systems.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the definition of stochastic energy measurements $X$ and $Y$. Figure 2 details the processes and quantities involved in deriving the detailed fluctuation theorem for work.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds upon Jarzynski's framework by deriving both detailed and integral fluctuation theorems. It then connects the averages of these fluctuations to nonadiabaticity and excess work, culminating in a discussion of the strongest attainable thermodynamic minimal work principle.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We expand on the ideas developed by C. Jarzynski in Physica A 552, 122077 (2020), where an integral fluctuation theorem was derived with the aim of obtaining thermodynamic inequalities stronger than those implied by the Jarzynski equality. Restricting ourselves to the quantum setting, we derive the corresponding detailed fluctuation theorem and additional detailed and integral fluctuation theorems; we also provide a clear physical interpretation of the stochastic quantities defined in the previous reference. Furthermore, we show that their averages are given by the nonadiabaticity parameter (i.e., the relative entropy between the final state after a finite-time driving protocol and the corresponding adiabatically evolved state) and the excess work (also known as inner friction). We elaborate on the inequalities derived from the fluctuation theorems and discuss their connection to irreversibility and formulations of the Second Law.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a novel higher-order quantum functional language designed to accurately model processes with Indefinite Causal Orders (ICOs), such as the quantum switch. It achieves this by defining its semantics within the causal category Caus[CPM], ensuring that every well-typed program corresponds to a physically realizable quantum process. This provides a powerful theoretical tool for advancing quantum computation beyond unitary constraints.

**Why it may be interesting.** For theoretical physicists working on open quantum systems or quantum computation, this work provides a mathematically rigorous tool to model non-unitary, context-dependent quantum operations (like those involving measurement) that are crucial for understanding realistic physical implementations beyond simple unitary evolution.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is developing a quantum programming language capable of faithfully modeling processes with Indefinite Causal Orders (ICOs), especially when these orders are combined with measurements, which existing languages fail to capture fully.

**Main result.** The authors introduce a higher-order functional language whose semantics are defined in the causal category Caus[CPM], proving that well-typed programs correspond to physically meaningful quantum processes and can express complex structures like the quantum switch.

**Method.** They develop both an operational (small-step) and denotational semantics, using advanced categorical tools (completely positive maps and the Causal Category construction) to rigorously enforce physical constraints beyond simple linearity.

**Model / system.** The model is a theoretical quantum computation framework dealing with general quantum channels and supermaps. Key concepts include ICOs, which allow superposition over operation orderings, and the necessity of tracking measurement outcomes across these superposed branches.

**Key observables.** Physical meaning of computed processes; successful synchronization of measurement outcomes across superposed computational paths.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The structure of quantum channels (CPTP maps) and the complexity level (first-order vs. second-order operations).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework assumes that physical reality can be modeled by processes whose semantics are defined within the causal category Caus[CPM], requiring a typing discipline beyond simple linearity.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the quantum switch program structure, the syntax of the extended language (including 'meas' and 'qcase'), and the formal definitions for both operational reduction rules and denotational semantic components.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from identifying the limitations of current languages to introducing a novel higher-order functional language. It then rigorously defines its semantics using advanced category theory, proves soundness between operational and denotational interpretations, and finally demonstrates the language's expressive power by implementing known quantum circuits with memory.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Processes with indefinite causal orders (ICOs), such as the quantum switch, are higher-order quantum processes that superpose the order in which quantum operations are performed. Such coherent control yields computational advantages but is not faithfully captured by existing quantum programming languages: either they are restricted to the unitary case, and thus cannot combine ICOs with measurement, or they treat coherent control nonlinearly. In both cases, they do not realize the full computational power of ICOs. We introduce a higher-order quantum functional language that supports general quantum computation, not merely the permutation of channels, and whose linear type system allows quantum control to be well-defined beyond the unitary case, on arbitrary quantum channels. We equip this language with a small-step operational semantics that synchronizes measurement outcomes across superposed branches, using device references and a memory function. We also give a denotational semantics by means of completely positive maps. With linearity as the only constraint, some well-typed terms would denote unphysical maps. We therefore impose a typing discipline that goes beyond linearity, and interpret programs in the causal category Caus[CPM], under which every well-typed program is physically meaningful, a property that can be checked statically and efficiently. We prove soundness, and study the language's expressive power: it can express every quantum channel at first order, and at second order a large subclass of the so-called quantum circuits with quantum control (QC-QCs), containing the quantum switch. Last but not least, we show that this language is well-designed enough to be extended to the nonlinear setting with recursion.

</details>

**Summary.** The research uses advanced DMRG simulations to map the quantum phase diagram of dipolar molecules confined in curved endofullerene chains. By varying the chain angle ($\gamma$), the system undergoes transitions between ferroelectric and antiferroelectric ordered states, demonstrating that geometry dictates complex magnetic ordering in nanoscale materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work connects fundamental concepts from quantum magnetism (Néel ordering, frustration) with materials science by proposing that engineered carbon nanostructures can host tunable dipole-ordered quantum phases.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the quantum phases of dipolar molecules confined within bent (zigzag) endofullerene chains as a function of the chain's geometric bending angle ($\gamma$).

**Main result.** The system exhibits rich dipole-ordered quantum phases, transitioning from ferroelectric to antiferroelectric order at $\gamma=60^\circ$, with specific molecular properties determining the stability of these orders.

**Method.** Large-scale Density Matrix Renormalization Group (DMRG) calculations are employed to analyze the ground state and phase transitions across varying chain geometries.

**Model / system.** The system models dipolar molecules ($	ext{LiF}$, $	ext{H}_2	ext{O}$) confined in quasi-1D zigzag chains formed by endofullerene cages. The physics is governed by dipole-dipole interactions and the geometric constraint imposed by the bending angle $\gamma$.

**Key observables.** Polarization (axial/scaled), Binder parameter ($U_z$), von Neumann entanglement entropy, and correlation functions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Chain angle ($\gamma$) ranging from $60^\circ$ to $180^\circ$; effective dipole moment ($\mu_{	ext{eff}}$); nearest-neighbor vs. next-nearest-neighbor coupling strength.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on treating the system via a Matrix Product State (MPS) representation suitable for DMRG calculations, and assumes that dipolar interactions are the dominant physics.

**Figures summary.** Figures likely show polarization measurements confirming ferroelectric alignment ($\cos	heta \approx 1$) at large $\gamma$ and antiferroelectric anti-alignment ($\cos	heta \approx -1$) at $\gamma=60^\circ$, alongside Binder cumulant plots locating the quantum phase transition.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes different molecular species ($	ext{LiF}$, $	ext{o-D}_2	ext{O}$) across a range of bending angles, using DMRG to map out transitions between ferroelectric and antiferroelectric ordered states, paying special attention to the critical role of NNN interactions at $60^\circ$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We employ large-scale density matrix renormalization group calculations to study the quantum phases of dipolar molecules confined in bent (zigzag) endofullerene chains, as a function of the chain angle $γ$. For LiF, ferroelectric order persists across the full range $60^\circ < γ180^\circ$, with the critical effective dipole moment increasing as the chain bends and parallel alignment becomes less favorable. Near the equilateral configuration ($γ= 60^\circ$), geometric frustration drives a transition to an antiferroelectric Néel-ordered phase in which neighboring dipoles anti-align along the chain axis. We show that capturing this reorientation requires including dipolar couplings beyond the nearest-neighbor approximation, since next-nearest-neighbor interactions become equally strong at $γ= 60^\circ$. For confined water, o-D$_2$O reproduces both ordered phases, whereas p-H$_2$O -- owing to its large rotational constants -- develops no order at any chain angle despite the enhanced coordination of the bent geometry. Because a zigzag chain is the narrowest stripe of a two-dimensional lattice, these results suggest that engineered endofullerene layers could host a rich variety of dipole-ordered quantum phases beyond the ferroelectric ordering observed in previous work.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors used inelastic neutron scattering and spin-wave theory to study $\alpha$-MnTe, an altermagnet. They demonstrated that applying a magnetic field can continuously rotate the material's Néel vector while leaving the intrinsic magnon frequencies unchanged. This establishes MnTe as a promising platform for reconfiguring magnonic interactions using external fields.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a clean platform for studying tunable coupling mechanisms in magnetic excitations, which is relevant to understanding how external fields can modulate quantum interactions (like magnon-photon coupling) without destroying the underlying quantum state's frequency characteristics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how an external magnetic field controls the Néel vector orientation and magnon visibility in altermagnetic $\alpha$-MnTe.

**Main result.** The research shows that applying a field reorients the Néel vector without significantly changing the magnon energies or line widths, demonstrating a clear separation between the soft orientational degree of freedom and the robust chiral magnon spectrum.

**Method.** The study combines linear spin-wave theory calculations with inelastic neutron scattering (INS) measurements to probe the magnetic excitations.

**Model / system.** The system is altermagnetic $\alpha$-MnTe, which exhibits spin splitting without net magnetization. The theoretical model uses a Heisenberg Hamiltonian augmented by an external Zeeman term ($\mathcal{H}_Z$).

**Key observables.** Magnon energies and line widths; spectral intensity measured via the transverse-momentum projection ($S_{\perp}^{xy}(Q, E)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The ratio between the exchange scale and the applied Zeeman energy ($\mathcal{H}_Z$) is crucial, with the field remaining small compared to the dominant exchange interaction.

**Assumptions / limitations.** It is assumed that secondary effects from Néel vector rotation dominate over direct Zeeman coupling to magnons due to the large exchange scale. Line widths are often noted as being resolution-limited.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the magnetic model and spin orientation change with field (Fig 1); INS measurements show magnon dispersion curves at different fields, confirming energy stability while intensity changes (Figs 3 & 4); Figure 5 quantifies the increase in integrated magnon intensity due to ground state reorientation.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical framework using spin-wave theory and then presents experimental INS data comparing zero-field and finite-field measurements. The core argument builds by showing that field changes primarily affect measurable intensity via projection rather than altering fundamental excitation frequencies or coherence times.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Altermagnetic order gives rise to momentum-dependent spin splitting of electronic and magnonic excitations even in the absence of a net magnetization. Here, we investigate the magnetic field dependence of the spin-wave spectrum of altermagnetic $α$-MnTe using inelastic neutron scattering and linear spin-wave theory. An in-plane magnetic field continuously reorients the Néel vector by overcoming the weak crystalline anisotropy, while remaining small compared with the dominant exchange scale. We find that this reorientation leaves the magnon energies and line widths essentially unchanged, but strongly modifies the measured spectral intensity through the transverse-momentum projection. Our results demonstrate a clear separation between the soft orientational degree of freedom of the antiferromagnetic order and the robust exchange-dominated chiral magnon spectrum. This combination establishes $α$-MnTe as a platform for reconfigurable magnon coupling, in which external fields tune how excitations interact with polarized probes without substantially altering their frequency or coherence.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops the theory of 'pinch-curve spin liquids,' where magnetic correlations exhibit singularities forming 1D curves in momentum space. By exploiting inversion symmetry, the constraints defining these curves are mathematically simplified and shown to be algebraically programmable. The research confirms that these systems undergo a novel Gauss-law transition while maintaining their singular geometric structure.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous mathematical framework for understanding emergent topological features (singular loci) in classical spin systems, linking algebraic geometry directly to physical correlation functions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper introduces 'pinch-curve spin liquids,' generalizing classical spin liquids where correlation singularities form one-dimensional algebraic curves in momentum space.

**Main result.** Symmetry protection reduces the singularity condition to two real polynomial constraints, allowing for algebraically programmable pinch loci that host an infrared Gauss-law transition.

**Method.** The analysis combines continuum theory using polynomial constraint equations with lattice simulations (Monte Carlo) on spin models derived from generalized Gauss's laws.

**Model / system.** Classical spin liquids are modeled by two-component scalar-charge Gauss's laws, leading to a Hamiltonian constrained by local operators in momentum space. The system is studied via its singular locus $\mathbf{Z}$ defined by the common zeros of these constraints.

**Key observables.** The structure factor $\langle S_{a}(\mathbf{k})S_{b}(-\mathbf{k})angle$ and the geometry of the pinch curve in momentum space ($\mathbf{k}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Inversion symmetry ($I$), lattice size ($L=50$), and the degree/homogeneity of the polynomial constraints.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often focuses on diagonal symmetry cases, simplifying the constraint to $P_1(\mathbf{k}) = P_2(\mathbf{k}) = 0$.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate geometric intersections in k-space (straight vs. curved lines) and compare structure factors from lattice models against zero-mode predictions; one figure shows the transition of the local constraint order.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining pinch-curve spin liquids via symmetry protection, deriving algebraic constraints ($P_1=P_2=0$), classifying the geometry based on polynomial forms (homogeneous vs. inhomogeneous), and finally validating these predictions using lattice Monte Carlo simulations and analyzing phase transitions.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Classical spin liquids are correlated paramagnets in which local constraints generate extensive degeneracy and emergent gauge structures, often observable as pinch-point singularities in spin structure factors. Here we introduce pinch-curve spin liquids, in which the pinch singularities form one-dimensional algebraic curves in momentum space. Inversion symmetry protects these curves by reducing the singularity condition to two real algebraic constraints in three dimensions, and the geometry of the pinch locus is algebraically programmable. We identify elementary mechanisms for generating straight and curved pinch loci, construct lattice spin models that realize them, and test the predicted structure factors using Monte Carlo simulations. We further show that pinch curves can host an infrared Gauss-law transition: the leading local constraint and the associated anisotropic scaling of the structure factor change, even though the singular locus remains one-dimensional.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper argues that standard eigenvalue methods fail to capture the true, finite-depth dynamics of attention mechanisms in large language models because these operators are non-normal. By employing advanced resolvent techniques, the authors demonstrate that transient behavior is governed by quantities like pseudospectra and Kreiss constants, revealing distinct functional roles for different parts of the network.

**Why it may be interesting.** The mathematical tools developed—using pseudospectra and Kreiss constants to analyze non-normal operators in a deep product structure—are directly applicable to understanding the stability and information flow dynamics in complex quantum systems or open quantum channels where simple spectral analysis fails.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study challenges whether eigenvalue analysis is sufficient or misleading for understanding the finite-depth dynamics of attention propagators in causal transformers, especially since these operators are non-normal.

**Main result.** Resolvent quantities (like pseudospectra and Kreiss constants) are necessary to predict depth-transient behavior, as eigenvalue predictions fail by many orders of magnitude on trained stacks. The analysis reveals distinct functional roles for different attention heads acting as transient dampers or amplifiers.

**Method.** The authors compare spectral properties derived from eigenvalues against resolvent quantities (pseudospectra, Kreiss constants) applied to the deflated attention operator product over depth.

**Model / system.** The system analyzed is the causal attention matrix of large language models (GPT-2, Pythia, Llama), which forms a row-stochastic, non-normal operator whose dynamics are governed by repeated multiplication across model depth.

**Key observables.** Kreiss constant ($K_{dev}$), pseudospectral radius ($ho_\epsilon$), transient reserve correlations, and the deviation of actual depth evolution from eigenvalue heuristics.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Model scale (e.g., Llama-3-8B), sequence length/depth ($L$), and the structural components like 'routing minority' vs. 'sink majority'.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is structured around pre-registered criteria assuming eigenvalue reasoning fails for finite depth, and all quantitative results are reported as excess over per-matrix nulls.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the failure of eigenvalue heuristics compared to measured deviation transients (orders of magnitude difference) and show how specific heads transition between sink/damper states based on context or probes.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the non-normality of the attention propagator, contrasts spectral vs. resolvent views, analyzes structural components (minority/majority), demonstrates quantitative failure of eigenvalues, and concludes that resolvent features are essential for predicting depth persistence.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The attention matrix of a causal transformer is row-stochastic, iterated over depth, and non-normal by construction. For non-normal operators, eigenvalues control only asymptotic behavior; finite-depth behavior is controlled by resolvent quantities such as pseudospectra and Kreiss constants. We test, under pre-registered criteria, whether this resolvent view predicts anything about trained transformers that eigenvalues miss. Two structural facts organize the analysis: the mask pins the Kreiss constant of every causal stochastic matrix at $\sqrt{n}$, and deflating the mask-forced Perron projector factorizes the depth deviation dynamics exactly into a product of deflated operators. Across GPT-2, Pythia-410m, and Llama-3-8B, learned non-normality proves to be signed. A routing minority carries excess transient reserve that tracks previous-token function and doubles when induction heads engage, while the sink majority is suppressed below matched shuffle nulls, so that attention sinks act as transient dampers. On depth products, eigenvalue predictions of surviving deviations err by seven to eleven orders of magnitude, an error absent in matched nulls. Checkpoint censuses date this organization to a consolidation phase after circuit formation, and a clamping intervention on Llama-3-8B establishes a causal chain from three massive activation dimensions through sink attention to transient damping; LayerNorm models implement the same functions elsewhere. A cross-validated contest concludes that resolvent features are required for depth-transient persistence and routing-head identity, and that no single-operator summary of any kind predicts per-head causal criticality.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces advanced semiclassical Gaussian wavepacket methods to simulate complex non-adiabatic molecular dynamics. The authors propose an improved variational approach (NA-VTGWD) which correctly models electronic state transitions by coupling classical trajectories on diabatic surfaces. This method proves superior to standard approximations, accurately reproducing quantum effects in systems like electron transfer and photo-dissociation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses non-adiabatic dynamics—a cornerstone of molecular physics and quantum chemistry—by providing a computationally tractable semiclassical framework that rigorously conserves fundamental quantities.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is accurately simulating non-adiabatic molecular dynamics, which requires methods beyond the Born-Oppenheimer approximation.

**Main result.** The proposed Variational Thawed Gaussian method (NA-VTGWD) successfully reproduces quantum mechanical results for various non-adiabatic processes while maintaining crucial physical symmetries like energy and norm conservation.

**Method.** The authors develop two semiclassical methods based on the thawed Gaussian ansatz: a standard formulation (NA-TGWD) and an improved variational version (NA-VTGWD) derived from time-dependent variational principles.

**Model / system.** The models involve coupled electronic states described by diabatic potential energy surfaces ($V_{11}, V_{22}$) with coupling terms ($V_{12}$). Tests include electron transfer using coupled harmonic oscillators and photo-dissociation modeled with a Morse potential.

**Key observables.** Diabatic state populations ($ho_{ii}(t)$), wavepacket density heatmaps, and conservation of total energy and norm.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The coupling strength ($V_{12}$), the nature of the potentials (harmonic vs. Morse), and the time evolution parameters for the Gaussian wavepackets.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Both methods rely on the thawed Gaussian ansatz, which is exact only for specific potential forms; the variational approach is superior because it guarantees adherence to the Schrödinger equation solution.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare population dynamics and wavepacket heatmaps across three approaches: exact quantum evolution (split-operator), NA-VTGWD, and the less accurate NA-TGWD for both coupled oscillators and Morse potential systems.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the general problem, details the two semiclassical methods (NA-TGWD in one section, NA-VTGWD in another), validates them using Rabi oscillations, and applies them to complex physical scenarios like electron transfer and photo-dissociation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We introduce two non-adiabatic semiclassical methods that employ two coupled Gaussian wavepackets, each one traveling on a separate diabatic potential energy surface. The wavepackets take the form of thawed Gaussians and are driven by classical equations of motion which account for the diabatic coupling. The classical equations of motion are derived in one case by enforcing the thawed Gaussian ansatz, while in the other the time-dependent variational principles to the thawed Gaussian ansatz. After a sanity check where both approximations reproduce Rabi oscillations, the methods are applied to two non-adiabatic potential energy scenarios. The first one involves two coupled displaced harmonic oscillators, as in a typical electron transfer reaction. The second one comprises a Morse potential coupled to an upper dissociative state, modeling a photo-dissociation process. In both scenarios, the variational thawed Gaussian approach is quite accurate, while the standard thawed Gaussian one fails to fully capture the non-adiabatic effects. Ultimately, non-adiabatic molecular dynamics is reproduced by means of two classical trajectories without introducing any artificial jump or other ad-hoc non-classical effects.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper provides a critical overview of strongly correlated fermion systems, centering on the $t$-$J$ model derived from the Hubbard Hamiltonian. It details advanced theoretical techniques like canonical perturbation expansion to derive effective Hamiltonians governing high-temperature superconductivity and heavy fermion behavior. Furthermore, it extends its scope by introducing 'atomicity' as a necessary correction for describing chemical bonding in molecules.

**Why it may be interesting.** The paper bridges solid-state physics (Hubbard/t-J models for high-$T_c$) with molecular physics ($	ext{H}_2$ bonding), providing a unified theoretical framework that addresses how correlation effects manifest differently in extended solids versus simple molecules. The concept of 'atomicity' is a novel diagnostic tool.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central problem is developing a comprehensive theory for strongly correlated fermion systems, particularly addressing high-temperature superconductivity and understanding the nature of chemical bonding in nanosystems.

**Main result.** The paper establishes the $t$-$J$ model as a fundamental framework by deriving an effective Hamiltonian through canonical perturbation expansion, while also introducing 'atomicity' to correct physical descriptions of molecular bonding.

**Method.** The methodology combines theoretical derivations using projection operators ($P_1, P_2$) on the Hubbard model, canonical perturbation theory, and analytical solutions for molecular Hamiltonians (Heitler-London type).

**Model / system.** The primary models are the Hubbard/t-J model for solid-state physics (cuprates, heavy fermions) and the extended Heitler-London model for diatomic molecules like H2. The analysis spans from lattice dynamics to molecular bonding.

**Key observables.** Superconducting pairing correlations ($\langle \hat{b}^\dagger_{ij} angle$), spin-dependent effective masses, Mott transition behavior, Covalency, Ionicity, and Atomicity in chemical bonds.

**Important parameters / regimes.** $U$ (Coulomb repulsion), $t_{ij}$ (hopping amplitude), bandwidth ($W$), interatomic distance ($R/a_0$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation relies on the strong correlation limit ($|t_{ij}| \ll U-K$) and the use of orthogonal projection operators to decompose the Fock space.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the Brillouin zone for a quantum Fermi liquid. Figure 18 shows electron density profiles for H2 at various interatomic distances, demonstrating the separation into atomic states as distance increases.

**Paper structure.** The structure moves from reviewing the historical development of strong correlation theory (Mott to t-J model) via Hamiltonian decomposition and perturbation expansion, to applying these concepts to specific physical regimes like superconductivity, and finally extending the formalism to analyze chemical bonding using the concept of atomicity.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

I critically overview my research on strongly correlated fermion systems for almost five decades. It concentrated on: (i) the first derivation of what is now called the t--J model, comprising both the limit of Anderson kinetic exchange of spin--spin interaction in the Mott--Hubbard insulator and taking into account real-space pairing, subsequently applied to high-temperature superconductivity; (ii) the concept of spin-dependent heavy mass of quasiparticles in heavy-fermion systems, and (iii) the first nontrivial model of statistical thermodynamics of the Mott--Hubbard transition. Those three features, together with the specific quantum critical phenomena provide, in my view, fundamental components of the theory of strongly correlated fermions established in the 1960s. Some related questions such as introduction of atomicity in the chemical bonding (iv), and specific properties of correlated nanosystems within the rigorous EDABI (v) approach are also briefly elaborated at the end.

</details>

### [Lean-QIT: Towards a Formal Infrastructure for Quantum Information Theory](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09632v1)

**Authors:** Chengkai Zhu, Ziao Tang, Guocheng Zhen, Yimeng Cao, Yusheng Zhao, Ranyiliu Chen, Xuanqiang Zhao, Lei Zhang, Xin Wang  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.09632v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This paper introduces Lean-QIT, a novel library built in the proof assistant Lean 4, designed to provide a machine-checkable foundation for Quantum Information Theory. It achieves this by creating reusable interfaces that formally connect operational coding protocols with deep analytic theorems like the classical capacity bounds. This infrastructure aims to enable automated reasoning and formal verification across quantum information science.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on abstract theory, the rigorous formalization of capacity theorems provides a foundational mathematical toolset that could be adapted to analyze resource constraints or information flow in complex quantum simulations.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is formalizing Quantum Information Theory (QIT) by unifying finite-block protocols, analytic inequalities, and asymptotic limits into a single, machine-checkable framework.

**Main result.** The authors successfully formalized major QST theorems—including Schumacher's source coding theorem and the HSW capacity theorem—within a reusable library structure that separates operational definitions from analytical characterizations.

**Method.** They developed Lean-QIT, a library in the proof assistant Lean 4. This method involves building composable, kernel-checked interfaces for quantum states, channels, and information measures to ensure mathematical rigor.

**Model / system.** The framework models finite-dimensional quantum systems using typed structures for quantum states ($ho$) and completely positive trace-preserving (CPTP) channels ($\Phi$). It handles subsystems, measurements (POVMs), and classical registers abstractly.

**Key observables.** Achievable rates, capacities (e.g., Holevo–Schumacher–Westmoreland capacity), error criteria, von Neumann entropy, and quantum relative entropy.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Error tolerance ($\epsilon$), blocklength ($n$), and the Rényi parameter ($\alpha$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The work assumes that complex QIT theorems can be decomposed into named, reusable components (e.g., direct/converse bounds) to build a compositional knowledge substrate.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the global architecture of Lean-QIT as a stack built on Mathlib, showing distinct layers for object calculus, information interfaces, and operational coding surfaces. Figure 2 details the layered proof structure from basic objects up to rate constructions.

**Paper structure.** The paper is structured around building an infrastructure: first defining typed quantum objects (states/channels), then implementing core information measures (entropy, testing), and finally assembling these components to prove major theorems by bridging finite-block results to asymptotic limits via 'squeezes'.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum information theory (QIT) characterizes the capabilities and fundamental limits of quantum information processing, underpinning quantum communication, computation, and error correction. Formalizing its coding theorems requires connecting finite-block protocols, analytic inequalities, and asymptotic limits within a unified machine-checked framework. Existing developments, however, lack a reusable operational layer that defines codes, error criteria, achievable rates, and capacities independently of their information-theoretic characterizations. In this work, we present LeanQIT, a Lean 4 library for finite-dimensional QIT. It provides composable, kernel-checked interfaces for quantum states and channels, source and channel codes, finite-block performance criteria, hypothesis testing, one-shot quantities, and asymptotic rate constructions. Using this infrastructure, we formalize Schumacher's quantum source-coding theorem, the Holevo--Schumacher--Westmoreland classical-capacity theorem, and the entanglement-assisted classical-capacity theorem together with its strong converse. By separating operational definitions from analytic characterizations and exposing reusable achievability, converse, and asymptotic components, Lean-QIT provides a machine-readable foundation for formal QIT and a compositional knowledge substrate for emerging AI-assisted formalization, automated proof search, and agentic reasoning in quantum information and computation.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents PCPOP.jl, a specialized Julia package designed to solve complex polynomial optimization problems in non-commutative settings relevant to quantum physics. It utilizes moment relaxation formulated as Semidefinite Programs (SDPs) and incorporates advanced algebraic tools for partially commutative variables. This provides a powerful computational tool for analyzing bounds on quantum correlations and information measures.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides essential mathematical machinery for tackling hard optimization problems arising from quantum correlations and entanglement measures, which are central to modern quantum information theory.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is polynomial optimization, extended to non-commutative settings like partially commutative variables, tracial, and state polynomial optimization.

**Main result.** The paper introduces PCPOP.jl, a Julia package that automates the construction of Semidefinite Programming (SDP) relaxations to approximate the true optimum value of these complex quantum optimization problems.

**Method.** It employs moment relaxation techniques, which formulate the problem as an SDP solvable via specialized algebraic reductions like Gröbner basis methods and canonical clique representations for partially commutative variables.

**Model / system.** The framework is designed for modeling physical constraints arising in quantum information theory, such as those related to Bell inequalities or general non-commutative polynomial constraints.

**Key observables.** The optimal value $p^*$ of the polynomial optimization problem, and diagnostic metrics like SDP size, number of variables, and solving time during benchmarking.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The degree bound ($d$) used in moment relaxations, which dictates the complexity and convergence rate; boundedness assumptions are crucial for convergence.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Convergence of the moment relaxation sequence $p^*_d$ to the true optimum $p^*$ requires assuming that the feasible set is bounded. Furthermore, specific conditions like 'flatness' are needed for trace polynomial optimization.

**Figures summary.** The notes summarize benchmarking results across four tables comparing PCPOP against other packages (Ncpol2sdpa, QuantumNPA, Moment) based on setup time and solving time for various quantum contextuality problems.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the general polynomial optimization framework, details the extensions to non-commutative/quantum settings, describes the computational tools within PCPOP.jl (including clique representation), and concludes with a performance comparison against existing state-of-the-art solvers.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Here we present PCPOP, a Julia package for polynomial optimization that supports non-commutative optimization, tracial polynomial optimization, trace polynomial optimization and state polynomial optimization. PCPOP fully supports exact arithmetic computations and incorporates convenient functionalities such as algebraic reductions based on Gröbner basis methods, automatized symmetrization via Wedderburn decompositions, and Jordan algebra reductions. As a distinguished feature, PCPOP implements a specialized framework for polynomial computations in partially commutative variables that provides significant computational advantages for problems appearing in quantum information.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper presents a general quantum scattering model for Dual Parallel Mach-Zehnder Modulators (DP-MZMs) to analyze their effect on single photons and coherent states. It quantifies how imperfections like bias error degrade the performance of these modulators when used as frequency-bin qubit sources. The results are crucial for designing high-fidelity photonic quantum information systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous quantum description of a key photonic component used in quantum communication protocols, directly impacting the feasibility assessment for building practical quantum hardware.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a comprehensive quantum-mechanical model for Dual Parallel Mach-Zehnder Electro-Optic Modulators (DP-MZMs) to accurately describe their action on non-classical light.

**Main result.** The model provides the complete, unrestricted unitary transformation for single-photon and coherent states, allowing quantification of performance degradation due to various physical impairments like bias error and I/Q imbalance.

**Method.** The analysis employs the quantum scattering formalism by deriving stage-by-stage unitary operators that compose the entire device action. This is applied separately to single-photon creation operators and displacement operators for coherent states.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a DP-MZM used as a frequency shifter/encoder in photonic quantum information. The model tracks the evolution of light fields through cascaded beamsplitters, phase modulators, and combiners using unitary scattering operators ($\hat{S}_{DP}$).

**Key observables.** Single-photon sideband click probability $|\hat{H}(q)|^2$, two-frequency-bin qubit fidelity ($F$), and image-sideband suppression ratio ($\varepsilon_{dB}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Modulation indices ($m_I, m_Q$), quadrature bias error ($\Delta\phi_P$), I/Q imbalance, and RF drive frequencies ($\Omega$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The model is general, accounting for non-ideal components; however, the final analysis often assumes classical RF drives (c-number parameters) or uses approximations like the BCH theorem.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the device layout and frequency comb structure. Numerical results show single-photon sideband probabilities under varying modulation indices and quantify qubit fidelity degradation versus bias error.

**Paper structure.** The paper derives the general unitary transformation for both single-photon and coherent inputs, analyzes specific operating points (push-pull vs. quadrature), and then numerically evaluates performance metrics like fidelity using Monte Carlo simulations to assess noise sensitivity.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a quantum mechanical model of dual parallel Mach Zehnder electro-optic modulators (DPMZMs), derived using the quantum scattering formalism [1,2]. We obtain the complete, unrestricted transformation of single-photon and coherent input states through the DPMZM architecture, including independent radiofrequency drive conditions on each internal modulator, and recover the symmetric push pull operating point as an exact special case. We numerically evaluate the resulting frequency comb structure, quantify the device sensitivity to bias error, modulation-index imbalance, and RF noise, and use the model to design a DP-MZM-based frequency-bin qubit source, reporting its fidelity as a function of these impairments.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a combinatorial framework to measure the 'distance' between quantum graph states. This distance metric is shown to be equivalent to a graph property called rank integrity, which measures how much two graphs can differ while keeping the rank of their combined adjacency matrix low. The work provides both efficient algorithms and hardness results for determining this structural parameter.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a deep mathematical link between graph structure theory (edit distances) and the resource quantification of quantum entanglement in network models, which is crucial for understanding computational limits in measurement-based quantum computation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a combinatorial framework to analyze clustering graph states, which is mathematically related to quantum network edit-distance problems.

**Main result.** It establishes that the ancilla integrity problem for graph states is equivalent to rank integrity, proving this latter problem is XP parameterized by $k$ but also W[1]-hard in $k$.

**Method.** The authors use concepts from graph theory (vertex-minors, local complementation) and algebraic methods over GF(2) to translate quantum state distance into computable graph properties.

**Model / system.** The primary model is the graph state $|	ext{G}angle$ on qubits, which represents a quantum network resource. The analysis focuses on how these states can be related or perturbed using limited quantum operations (Clifford gates and Pauli measurements).

**Key observables.** The minimum number of ancilla qubits required to prepare two graph states, the maximum component size of the resulting cluster state $G'$, and the rank of the sum of adjacency matrices over GF(2).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The parameter $k$, which quantifies the allowed 'distance' or perturbation level between graph states.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The distance measure is restricted to preparation using one-qubit Clifford gates, Pauli measurements, and classical communication.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates a graph $G'$ obtained from $G$ by flipping, and the resulting sum graph $G + G'$, connecting structural changes to rank properties.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the distance metric, relates it combinatorially to vertex-minors, establishes equivalence with rank integrity, and then proves complexity results (XP vs. W[1]-hardness) for solving this parameterized problem.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We introduce a new notion of distance between two graph states $|G\rangle$ and $|G'\rangle$ on the same set of qubits. This distance is the minimum number of ancilla qubits in a graph state $|\widehat{G}\rangle$ from which both $|G\rangle$ and $|G'\rangle$ can be ``easily prepared''. (When preparing graph states, we are only allowed to use one-qubit Clifford gates, one-qubit Pauli measurements, and classical communication.) We give a graphical description of this distance through the lens of vertex-minors. We then show how this distance yields quantum network analogs of many graph edit-distance problems.   Using this framework, we develop classical algorithms for identifying the ``highly entangled clusters'' of a graph state $|G\rangle$. The ancilla integrity problem asks, given a graph $G$ and integer $k$, for the minimum -- over all graph states $|G'\rangle$ with distance at most $k$ from $|G\rangle$ -- of the maximum component size of $G'$. Up to a factor of $2$ in the number of ancilla qubits, this problem is equivalent to rank integrity, where the distance between $G$ and $G'$ is instead the minimum rank of the sum of their adjacency matrices over $\text{GF}(2)$. We prove that rank integrity is XP parameterized by $k$. We also prove the complementary hardness result that rank integrity is W[1]-hard in $k$. Finally, we give an explicit $\mathcal{O}(n^6)$-time algorithm for ancilla integrity when $G$ has $n$ vertices and $k=1$.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces Orthogonal Quantum Krylov Diagonalization (OQKD), an algorithm designed to compute low-energy spectra of quantum systems by mimicking the stable Lanczos procedure. OQKD achieves this orthogonality directly on the quantum computer, bypassing the need for numerically unstable overlap matrix calculations inherent in prior methods. This establishes a robust and scalable framework for both eigensolving and state preparation protocols like Quantum Phase Estimation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a significant algorithmic improvement for quantum eigensolvers by directly addressing the fundamental stability bottleneck (overlap matrix regularization) that plagues current quantum subspace methods, making it highly relevant for simulating complex Hamiltonians in AMO physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is developing a quantum algorithm for finding low-energy spectra of many-body Hamiltonians that maintains the numerical stability and orthogonality properties of classical Lanczos methods without requiring overlap-matrix regularization.

**Main result.** The Orthogonal Quantum Krylov Diagonalization (OQKD) framework successfully implements an orthogonal analog of the Lanczos algorithm, eliminating the need for overlap matrix inversion. Furthermore, a restarted protocol is proposed that maintains convergence while keeping state preparation probabilities affordable.

**Method.** The authors reformulate the classical Lanczos recursion at the operator level, utilizing Generalized Quantum Signal Processing (GQSP) and block encoding to generate an orthonormal Krylov basis directly on the quantum computer.

**Model / system.** The method is tested using numerical simulations of the J1–J2 Heisenberg model. The core mathematical structure involves projecting the Hamiltonian onto a reduced subspace spanned by the Krylov basis vectors.

**Key observables.** Overlap matrix condition number ($\kappa(S)$), energy error convergence, and state preparation fidelity/success probability.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Subspace dimension ($D$), Lanczos coefficients ($\alpha_k, eta_k$), and the degree of polynomial transformation required for state preparation.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on establishing recurrence relations for Lanczos coefficients and utilizing the GQSP framework to implement high-degree polynomial transformations efficiently.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare QKD/CQKD (showing ill-conditioned overlap matrices) against OQKD, demonstrating that OQKD maintains orthogonality. Other figures show OQKD reproducing classical convergence and illustrating the performance of the restarted state-preparation protocol.

**Paper structure.** The paper first identifies the limitations of standard quantum Krylov methods due to non-orthogonality. It then introduces OQKD by reformulating the Lanczos recursion at the operator level, detailing its implementation via GQSP. Finally, it presents a restarted state-preparation protocol and validates the entire framework using numerical simulations on the J1–J2 model.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum subspace-diagonalization methods, particularly Quantum Krylov Diagonalization (QKD), provide a promising route for computing low-energy spectra of quantum many-body Hamiltonians. However, existing quantum Krylov approaches rely on non-orthogonal Krylov bases, requiring overlap-matrix regularization that limits numerical stability and accuracy. In this work, we introduce an Orthogonal Quantum Krylov Diagonalization (OQKD) framework that reformulates the classical Lanczos recursion at the operator level, enabling an orthogonal quantum implementation of Krylov-subspace diagonalization. By expressing Lanczos vectors as polynomial transformations of the Hamiltonian, OQKD reproduces the orthogonality, tridiagonal structure, and convergence behavior of the classical Lanczos algorithm thus eliminating the need for overlap-matrix regularization. We further show that the required Lanczos polynomials can be implemented using block encoding and Generalized Quantum Signal Processing with the same asymptotic query complexity as Chebyshev-based QKD methods. Numerical simulations of the $J_1$--$J_2$ Heisenberg model confirm the classical Lanczos convergence and numerical stability of the proposed method, while the measurement-complexity scaling is established analytically. Building upon the OQKD framework, we then introduce a restarted state-preparation protocol that replaces a single high-degree polynomial transformation with a sequence of fixed low-degree transformations, maintaining an affordable block encoding success probability while retaining comparable convergence. These results establish OQKD as an orthogonal quantum analog of the classical Lanczos algorithm and identify the restarted protocol as a promising state-preparation strategy for Quantum Phase Estimation.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates level statistics in generalized Rosenzweig–Porter models, minimal models for chaos crossover. Using advanced techniques like free probability theory, the authors derive a universal scaling form for full counting statistics in the fractal phase ($1 < \gamma < 2$). The findings confirm universality across model variations and provide deep insights into spectral correlations in quantum many-body systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses spectral properties in systems exhibiting intermediate quantum dynamics (fractal eigenstates), which is crucial for understanding the transition between integrable and chaotic regimes in complex many-body Hamiltonians relevant to AMO physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study focuses on determining the level statistics within the fractal phase of generalized Rosenzweig–Porter (RP) random matrix models.

**Main result.** The full counting statistics exhibit a simple, universal scaling form around the Thouless energy ($E_T$), which is shared across various generalizations of the RP model.

**Method.** The authors employ free probability theory and the replica method to analytically compute the full counting statistics in the large system size limit.

**Model / system.** The core system is the generalized Rosenzweig–Porter (RP) random matrix ensemble, which models the crossover between integrability and chaos. The Hamiltonian structure involves a diagonal random part ($A$) and a coupling term ($B$), allowing generalizations to Wigner or orthogonally invariant matrices.

**Key observables.** Full counting statistics, level compressibility, and generalized Inverse Participation Ratios ($I_q$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The phase is controlled by the parameter $\gamma$, defining regimes: fully delocalized ($\gamma < 1$), intermediate fractal ($1 < \gamma < 2$), and localized ($\gamma > 2$). The Thouless energy ($E_T$) characterizes the crossover.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that level compressibility is a superior diagnostic tool compared to raw level spacings, especially in non-uniform density of states regimes. A key comparison is made with the Quantum Random Energy Model (QREM).

**Figures summary.** Figure B1 illustrates the rate function $\Phi_N(k)$ for finite $N$ versus the asymptotic limit, confirming convergence properties.

**Paper structure.** The paper analyzes the RP model's phase diagram using advanced statistical tools. It calculates full counting statistics via free probability theory and validates these analytical results through large-deviation algorithms and numerical diagonalization, contrasting findings with simpler models like QREM.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Rosenzweig--Porter (RP) random matrix ensemble has emerged as a minimal model for the integrability-to-chaos crossover in quantum many-body systems. Its phase diagram features a region with fractal eigenstates, exhibiting intermediate spectral and localization properties between the fully localized and fully delocalized regimes. In this work, we explore several generalizations of the RP model and determine their level statistics at the scale of the Thouless energy $E_T$, which characterizes the crossover. Using tools from free probability theory and the replica method, we compute the full counting statistics in the limit of large system size, and show that it takes a simple, universal scaling form around $E_T$, shared across all variations of the model. We validate our analytical predictions using exact numerical diagonalization of large samples, and large-deviation algorithms that resolve the full counting statistics down to probabilities as low as $10^{-40}$. We also contrast our predictions with measurements on the quantum random energy model, which is the simplest model displaying many-body localization.

</details>
