**Summary.** This paper develops a complete ultrametric structure on the space representing different 'worlds' or sectors arising from infinite tensor products. By defining metrics like the decoherence exponent ($	ilde{d}$), it provides a quantitative measure of how rapidly distinct branches become operationally distinguishable under unitary evolution. This offers a rigorous mathematical underpinning for interpreting quantum branching in the context of environmental monitoring.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous mathematical tool (ultrametrics) to quantify the emergence of apparent state reduction and environmental monitoring effects in quantum theory, which is highly relevant for understanding open quantum systems dynamics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To mathematically formalize the concept of 'branching worlds' within quantum mechanics by defining a rigorous metric structure on von Neumann's incomplete tensor products.

**Main result.** The set of equivalence classes of these tensor product sectors forms a complete ultrametric space, providing a quantitative measure for state separation during unitary evolution.

**Method.** The authors define two metrics ($d$ and $	ilde{d}$) based on convergence exponents derived from inner products between sequence representatives. They prove the properties of these metrics to establish the required mathematical structure.

**Model / system.** The model is an infinite tensor product of Hilbert spaces, used as a mathematical framework for describing the Many-Worlds Interpretation (Everettian branching). The dynamics are governed by product unitaries acting on these sectors.

**Key observables.** The primary observables are the two metrics: $d$ (phase-sensitive) and $	ilde{d}$ (decoherence exponent), which quantify the rate of operational distinguishability between branches.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The convergence exponents ($p$) defining the metric, and the structure of the product unitaries $U(\otimes \varphi_i)$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes a mathematical framework where sectors are labeled by equivalence classes ($\Gamma$), and it explicitly notes that it does not solve all conceptual difficulties of the Everett interpretation.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the pseudo-ultrametric $d$ on $\Gamma$, proving its completeness after quotienting by the zero-distance relation to form a true ultrametric space. It then develops the gauge-invariant metric $	ilde{d}$ and applies it to analyze how product unitaries cause displacement, interpreting this as decoherence.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We revisit von Neumann's theory of infinite tensor products of Hilbert spaces. On the set $Γ$ of equivalence classes of $C_0$-sequences, which labels the incomplete tensor products inside the complete tensor product, we introduce a natural pseudo-ultrametric $d$: the distance between two classes is the convergence exponent of the series $\sum_j|\langle\varphi_j,ψ_j\rangle-1|$ formed from any pair of representatives. We show that $d$ is well defined on equivalence classes, satisfies the strong triangle inequality, and is complete. Distinct classes may lie at distance zero, so $d$ separates points only after passing to the quotient $\widetildeΓ$ of $Γ$ by the relation $d=0$; the pair $(\widetildeΓ,d)$ is then a complete ultrametric space. As an application, we show that a product unitary $\bigotimes_j U$ whose factor $U$ satisfies $\inf_{\|x\|=1}|\langle x,Ux\rangle-1|>0$ (in particular, a unitary on a finite dimensional space with $1\notinσ(U)$) displaces every class to the maximal distance $1$. Guided by the intended application -- a caricature of Everettian branching, in which the sectors of the infinite tensor product play the role of worlds -- we also develop a gauge-invariant variant $\tilde d$ of the metric, based on von Neumann's weak equivalence and matched to the quasi-equivalence of product states on the quasi-local algebra. The displacement of a class under a product unitary, measured by $\tilde d$, is class dependent and realizes every value in $[0,1]$. We interpret $\tilde d$ as a decoherence exponent: it measures the polynomial rate at which two branches of the universal state vector become operationally distinct as ever larger portions of the environment are monitored.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces 'local determinacy' as an alternative view of open quantum system dynamics, suggesting state evolution depends only on its current state rather than its entire history. By analyzing models like the Jaynes-Cummings system, the authors show that complex non-Markovian behavior can be mathematically recast into simple, autonomous second-order differential equations analogous to classical mechanics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the mathematical structure of non-Markovian dynamics in open quantum systems, providing a powerful, physically intuitive interpretation (mechanical analogy) for complex time evolution that goes beyond standard Lindblad or NZ formalisms.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The authors propose a novel framework for open quantum systems where state evolution is determined locally by its instantaneous behavior, challenging traditional non-Markovian descriptions that rely on the system's entire past history.

**Main result.** They demonstrate that local determinacy holds for several models, showing that complex non-Markovian dynamics can be mapped onto autonomous second-order differential equations structurally equivalent to classical mechanical systems.

**Method.** The analysis involves deriving higher-order differential equations governing the reduced density matrix trajectory and employing coordinate transformations to map these quantum dynamics onto known forms of classical mechanics (Lagrangian/Hamiltonian formalisms).

**Model / system.** The study analyzes two models: a multi-mode Jaynes–Cummings system coupled to harmonic oscillators, and an infinite central spin system. The dynamics are governed by master equations that exhibit non-Markovian behavior.

**Key observables.** Reduced trajectories $ho(t)$ (represented in Bloch vector form), which obey specific differential equations.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The coupling strengths ($g_k$), oscillator frequencies ($\omega_k$), and parameters defining the spectral density ($J(\omega)$) are crucial; regimes like $2\gamma_0 > \lambda$ indicate failure of simple time-local descriptions.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The existence proof for the differential equation relies on the total Hamiltonian having a finite point spectrum, and the system being finite dimensional in certain contexts. The method also requires specific coordinate charts for simplification.

**Figures summary.** Not specified by notes; however, the analysis involves deriving equations (e.g., Eq. 14, Eq. 23) that govern the local evolution of the reduced state variables $\gamma(t)$.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the concept of local determinacy for master equations, contrasting it with NZ approaches. It then applies this framework to the multi-mode JC model and subsequently to an infinite central spin system, deriving ODEs and establishing mechanical analogies.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We propose a novel formulation of master equations for open systems wherein the evolution of a state is determined solely by its local behaviour at any point of time. Specifically, our formulation allows for a local interpretation of the workings of beyond-Markovian dynamics as opposed to the more common conception that non-Markovian state evolution is affected by its cumulative past history. Quite interestingly, local determinacy is found prevalent in quantum dynamics. We illustrate the advantages of our coordinate-free formulation with exact analyses on two physically relevant models.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proposes using a non-Gaussian entanglement distillation technique, specifically Photon Addition–Photon Subtraction (PA-PS), to boost the performance of continuous-variable quantum teleportation over satellite links. By mitigating channel degradation caused by atmospheric loss and turbulence, the method significantly improves both fidelity and entanglement measures in realistic communication scenarios.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses practical limitations in quantum communication channels by integrating advanced quantum resource enhancement techniques (entanglement distillation) with detailed physical propagation models relevant to free-space optics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Quantum teleportation over satellite-based free-space optical channels suffers significant performance degradation due to atmospheric loss and turbulence.

**Main result.** Employing a non-Gaussian entanglement distillation protocol (PA-PS) significantly improves teleportation fidelity by up to 7.7% and enhances entanglement negativity by about 105% in low-to-moderate loss regimes.

**Method.** The authors model the process using continuous-variable quantum information theory, applying sequential Photon Addition–Photon Subtraction (PA-PS) distillation on the weaker channel before executing the Braunstein-Kimble teleportation protocol.

**Model / system.** The system is a dual-downlink Free Space Optical (FSO) link connecting ground stations to a satellite. The resource state is modeled as a Two-Mode Squeezed Vacuum (TMSV) state, and the channel degradation incorporates diffraction loss, atmospheric attenuation, and turbulence effects.

**Key observables.** Teleportation fidelity ($\langle F angle$), entanglement negativity ($N(ho_{AB})$), heralded success probability ($	ext{p}_{	ext{succ}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Squeezing parameter ($r$), satellite altitude ($H$), zenith angle ($	heta$), and various channel transmissivities ($	au$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the ability to apply PA-PS distillation selectively to the weaker of the two downlink channels, and it models complex physical losses like diffraction and turbulence.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the dual-downlink FSO quantum link setup and show how the CV-QT process incorporates the selection of the weaker link for PA–PS distillation.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the problem (lossy satellite QT), then details the physical modeling of channel degradation (diffraction, turbulence), introduces the PA-PS distillation protocol applied to the weakest link, and finally calculates the resulting fidelity and entanglement improvements compared to non-distilled scenarios.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum teleportation (QT) over satellite-based free-space optical (FSO) channels is a promising approach for long-distance quantum communication. However, its performance is significantly degraded by atmospheric loss and turbulence. In this paper, we investigate continuous-variable (CV) QT in a dual-downlink scenario, where a satellite distributes entangled states to two ground stations. To mitigate channel-induced degradation, we employ a non-Gaussian entanglement distillation protocol based on the sequential application of photon addition and photon subtraction (PA-PS) on the weaker channel. The results show that the proposed scheme improves teleportation fidelity by up to 7.7% and enhances entanglement negativity by approximately 105% in the low-to-moderate (below 600 km) loss regime. In addition, we identify an optimal squeezing parameter that balances entanglement strength and noise sensitivity. Taken together, these results demonstrate the effectiveness of PA-PS distillation for improving CV quantum communication in realistic satellite networks. We further characterize the trade-off between fidelity gain and the heralded success probability of the protocol.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces and theoretically analyzes Superconducting Singlet-Triplet (SST) qubits, which are promising candidates for scalable quantum processors because they avoid the need for spin-orbit coupling. The authors derive the effective Hamiltonian by treating the system in the transmon regime and utilize advanced perturbation theory to map out robust control protocols for single and two-qubit gates.

**Why it may be interesting.** The detailed derivation of an effective Hamiltonian from a complex superconducting circuit model using advanced many-body techniques like Schrieffer-Wolff is highly relevant to understanding controllable quantum hardware architectures in condensed matter physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper proposes superconducting singlet-triplet (SST) qubits as a robust alternative to Andreev spin qubits for quantum computation.

**Main result.** These SST qubits maintain the advantages of coupling to circuit QED while crucially eliminating the need for spin-orbit interaction, making them compatible with a wider range of materials like silicon.

**Method.** The effective qubit Hamiltonian is derived using Schrieffer-Wolff transformations and by analyzing the system in the transmon regime. Control mechanisms are mapped out based on manipulating superconducting phases ($\phi_j$).

**Model / system.** The physical platform consists of parallel-aligned double quantum dots integrated into Josephson junctions, forming an array of $N$ qubits coupled to a superconducting circuit. The Hamiltonian incorporates terms for charging energy ($E_C$), Josephson coupling ($E_J$), and crossed Andreev couplings ($E_{CA}$).

**Key observables.** Dispersive shift ($\chi_n(s)$) and Lamb shift ($\Lambda_n(s)$) are key observables used to characterize qubit-cavity coupling for readout.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis relies on regimes where $\Gamma, |\delta h| \ll |\epsilon| \ll U, \Delta$, and specifically assumes the transmon regime ($E_c \ll E_J$) for effective Hamiltonian derivation.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Key assumptions include operating in the dispersive regime for readout ($\omega_r - \omega_{tr} \gg g$), treating Zeeman coupling as a small perturbation, and neglecting higher-order terms during the Schrieffer-Wolff expansion.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the single SST qubit structure, while Figure 2 shows the array setup for $N$ qubits. The text references Hamiltonian equations (Eqs. 3, 4, 5) and cavity coupling details (Eqs. 7, 8).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the physical system and proposing SST qubits over Andreev qubits, to deriving the full system Hamiltonian, simplifying it via Schrieffer-Wolff transformations in the transmon regime, and finally detailing control schemes for single-qubit gates (X/Z set points) and two-qubit entangling gates.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Hybrid devices integrating quantum dots with Josephson junctions are gaining interest because they combine spin-based quantum computing with circuit quantum electrodynamics (circuit QED) methods. In particular, Andreev spin qubits have shown significant experimental progress including strong two-qubit coupling, and are predicted to exhibit all-to-all connectivity. Here we propose superconducting singlet-triplet (SST) qubits that rely on parallel-aligned double quantum dots in Josephson junctions. While Andreev spin qubits require spin-orbit interaction to unlock the spin degree-of-freedom, SST qubits do not require spin-orbit interaction, making the advantages of hybrid devices available to a wider range of materials. Similar to Andreev spin qubits, the qubit states couple to the superconducting phase across the junction, which allows for control and readout using circuit QED, and supports all-to-all connectivity. Only $N$ flux lines are required to perform any single- and two-qubit gate among $N$ qubits, and thus the overhead of control lines is small. Finally, linear protection from charge or flux noise makes these qubits interesting candidates for a future quantum processor.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper investigates the optimality of the free time evolution for a quantum wave function when its initial and final probability densities are fixed. By formulating this as an optimal transport problem, it shows that while the evolution is globally optimal without nodes, the presence of nodes renders the minimum only local, suggesting inherent instability in such configurations.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a deep variational characterization of free quantum evolution by linking it to optimal transport theory. The distinction between global minima (no nodes) and local minima (nodes present) offers novel insights into the stability of quantum states in open or constrained systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central problem is determining whether the free evolution of a quantum wave function, constrained by fixed initial and final probability densities, corresponds to an optimal path in a variational sense.

**Main result.** The free evolution minimizes a generalized action functional (related to quadratic optimal transport cost) when no nodes are present; however, the presence of nodes suggests intrinsic instability for this minimum.

**Method.** The analysis uses techniques from Optimal Transport Theory and Stochastic Mechanics, formulating the problem as minimizing an action functional $AQ(ho, v)$ derived from necessary conditions for extremality (Hamilton-Jacobi equations).

**Model / system.** The system is governed by the free Schrödinger equation in finite dimensions. The mathematical framework relates the quantum evolution to a diffusion process whose density $ho$ and current $v$ are analyzed.

**Key observables.** Probability density $ho(x, t) = |\psi(x, t)|^2$, associated velocity field $v(x, t)$, and the action functional $AQ(ho, v)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The time interval $[0, 1]$ and the boundary conditions defined by fixed initial ($ho_0$) and final ($ho_1$) moduli.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Assumes $\psi$ is unique up to a constant phase; handling nodes requires restricting analysis to a subset $\Xi$ where strong solutions of the associated SDE exist.

**Figures summary.** Not specified by notes, but the structure suggests proofs involving functional minimization and convergence arguments using sequences of approximations.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by defining the relevant state space ($\Xi$), deriving necessary conditions for extremality via calculus of variations (leading to HJM equations), establishing the connection to stochastic processes, and finally proving the optimality theorem through convex functional analysis and limiting procedures.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Extending and refining a preceding work, the free evolution of a quantum wave function, with fixed initial and final modulus, is considered in the general case where nodes are allowed. The method is based on considering a suitable simple functional related to the quadratic optimal transport cost in discrete time. Under reasonable assumptions it is shown that, if nodes are present, the free evolution of the wave function with fixed initial and final modulus is a sort of local minimum. If no nodes are present the minimum is global, suggesting intrinsic instability of the nodes in a free quantum evolution.

</details>

### [Playing Bayesian games better with separable quantum states than with any classical correlation](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09477v1)

**Summary.** This theoretical paper explores quantum advantages in Bayesian games by comparing equilibria derived from classical versus separable quantum correlations. It proves that these local quantum states can achieve better outcomes than any classically correlated strategy, even when the game involves conflicting interests. This advances the understanding of non-classical resources beyond simple entanglement.

**Why it may be interesting.** While not directly about AMO systems, the rigorous mathematical tools developed for quantifying non-local correlations in game theory are highly relevant to understanding resource theories in open quantum systems or multipartite entanglement verification.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research investigates how different types of correlations (classical vs. quantum) affect the equilibria achieved by independent agents in Bayesian games of incomplete information.

**Main result.** The paper demonstrates that even separable quantum states can generate new, genuinely quantum equilibria in competitive Bayesian games that yield higher social welfare than any equilibrium achievable using only classical correlations.

**Method.** The analysis uses game theory frameworks (Nash, Correlated Equilibria) combined with quantum information tools to compare the set of possible joint probability distributions derived from local vs. quantum measurements.

**Model / system.** The model is a general Bayesian game involving multiple players and incomplete information about their types. The correlation structure is modeled using quantum states (separable vs. entangled) acting on the players' shared Hilbert space.

**Key observables.** Social Welfare (SW), expected utility for individual players, and the comparison of equilibrium sets ($	ext{Corr}(G)$ vs. $	ext{QuSEP}(G)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The structure of the game itself, including payoff functions and prior distributions $p(t)$. Specific examples use parameters related to CHSH inequalities.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption is that separable quantum correlations are sufficient to demonstrate a quantum advantage in competitive settings, moving beyond previous reliance on full entanglement.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare various equilibrium scores (e.g., CHSH scores) against classical limits ($eta(G)$), showing separations between correlated and Nash equilibria when quantum strategies are employed.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds by first establishing the general framework of Bayesian games, then comparing correlation types (classical vs. quantum), and finally applying these concepts to specific examples like modified CHSH games to quantify the advantage.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Bayesian games, also known as games of incomplete information, are a fruitful arena for exploring the impact of correlations on a set of independent agents (players) via the game equilibria to which they give rise. It was realised some time ago that quantum states shared between the players can lead to new and beneficial equilibria, compared to classical correlation. While until now examples of this effect required an entangled state, here we show that even separable states can create new, genuinely quantum equilibria in games, that are advantageous with respect to all classically correlated equilibria. This shows that non-classical correlations beyond entanglement are indeed a resource, even in otherwise entirely classical situations. Our result brings quantum advantage in games significantly closer to possible realisation.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proposes a novel Quantum Identity Authentication protocol leveraging the unique noise reduction features of single-mode squeezed light. By implementing a four-direction squeezing scheme, the authors significantly boost security against eavesdropping attacks compared to existing methods. The analysis confirms that this approach provides quantifiable security advantages while remaining practical for implementation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant to quantum optics and open quantum systems as it provides a concrete, theoretically rigorous protocol for secure quantum key distribution/authentication using non-Gaussian resources (squeezed light) whose noise properties are precisely modeled.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a highly secure Quantum Identity Authentication (QIA) protocol that resists eavesdropping by exploiting non-classical properties of squeezed light.

**Main result.** The proposed four-direction squeezing approach halves the eavesdropper's guessing probability and increases the fidelity gap by 29% compared to binary methods, enhancing security without extra hardware.

**Method.** Security is analyzed using both a fidelity parameter (F) for detecting spoofing attacks and a secret information ratio ($\Delta I$) derived from mutual information calculations to quantify resistance against eavesdropping.

**Model / system.** The system utilizes single-mode light fields in the form of quadrature squeezed coherent states, measured via balanced homodyne detection. The protocol involves encoding secrets using squeezing parameters ($r$) and angles ($	heta$).

**Key observables.** Fidelity parameter (F), Secret Information Ratio ($\Delta I$), Quadrature variances $\langle[\Delta\hat{X}(\varphi)]^2angle$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Squeezing parameter ($r$), transmission efficiency ($\eta$), amplifier gain ($G$), and the number of squeezing directions (four-directional).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The protocol assumes Alice acts as a reliable server, Bob is the client, and security analysis relies on assuming independent/uniform selection of eavesdropper angles for averaging.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate phase-space representations of light states (vacuum, squeezed vacuum), schematic setups for QIA, and plots showing how security metrics ($\Delta F$ or $\Delta I$) are enhanced by increasing squeezing ($r$) or using the quaternary basis.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the need for secure QIA, details the physical model using quadrature squeezed states, develops the authentication mechanism involving key encoding and measurement, analyzes security via fidelity and mutual information ratios, and concludes with performance comparisons showing the advantage of the four-direction approach.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum identity authentication (QIA) has emerged as a crucial technology for secure communication systems, particularly in the burgeoning era of quantum communications. This paper proposes a novel QIA protocol based on non-classical characteristics of squeezed light fields. By exploiting quantum noise reduction properties of quadrature squeezed coherent states, the protocol fundamentally thwarts eavesdropping attempts by Heisenberg-limited uncertainty constraints. The fidelity parameter for decoy states is utilized to detect spoofing attacks, and the dynamic key update mechanism fundamentally eliminates vulnerabilities caused by key reuse. Security information ratio analysis shows that the protocol is able to resist Gaussian-cloner attacks and detect eavesdropping. Moreover, the security threshold can be further enhanced with higher squeezing, allowing tunable protection levels adaptable to different threat scenarios. Compared with binary-squeezed protocols, our proposed four-direction (quaternary-dimensional) squeezing halves the eavesdropper's guessing probability and enlarges the fidelity gap by 29% tightening the discrimination threshold without relying on extra hardware, thus facilitating practical implementation.

</details>

**Authors:** Lucas C. Céleri, Diogo O. Soares-Pinto, Daniel A. Turolla Vanzella  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.08819v1>

**Summary.** The authors derive a universal constraint showing that preparing large spatial superpositions of massive particles inevitably entangles the particle with the momentum recoil of the apparatus used for splitting. This entanglement causes decoherence in the measured particle state, setting a fundamental lower bound on experimental feasibility based only on conservation laws.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a fundamental, model-independent constraint on quantum metrology experiments involving massive objects, linking macroscopic mechanics (momentum conservation) directly to microscopic quantum coherence limits. It is crucial reading for anyone designing next-generation matter-wave interferometers or gravity tests.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper identifies a universal constraint on preparing massive particles in coherent spatial superpositions arising purely from momentum conservation when an apparatus is used for splitting.

**Main result.** Momentum conservation necessitates that the particle becomes entangled with the apparatus due to recoil, leading to a quantitative lower bound on achievable coherence even for macroscopic setups.

**Method.** The analysis uses quantum state evolution operators and calculates the reduced density matrix of the particle by tracing out the apparatus degrees of freedom, quantifying coherence via relative entropy.

**Model / system.** The system involves a massive particle ($m$) placed in superposition ($\pm d/2$) using an auxiliary apparatus of mass $M$. The analysis focuses on the entanglement generated between the particle and the apparatus's Center-of-Mass (CM) motion, modeled as harmonic oscillators.

**Key observables.** Coherence factor ($\chi_c$), relative entropy of coherence ($C_{rel}$), and the resulting bound relating $m$, $d$, $M$, and $T$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Particle mass ($m$), separation distance ($d$), apparatus mass ($M$), temperature ($T$), and characteristic frequencies ($\omega$). The low-temperature limit yields a temperature-independent constraint.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis primarily assumes that only the CM degree of freedom of the apparatus participates in state preparation, ignoring internal vibrational modes (which would tighten the bound).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate how coherence decreases with increasing particle mass and show contour plots mapping the momentum-conservation constraint on achievable superposition separation ($d/\lambda_C$) based on apparatus parameters.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the fundamental conservation law, derives the entanglement consequence for the reduced state, quantifies the resulting decoherence bound using CM motion, and finally discusses practical implications, including internal mode excitations and conditions for 'false' coherence loss.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Preparing massive particles in coherent spatial superpositions is a central objective of modern quantum science, motivated by applications ranging from fundamental tests of quantum mechanics and gravity to quantum-enhanced sensing. The experimental difficulty of realizing such superpositions is usually attributed to environmental decoherence mechanisms whose impact depends on the details of the experimental implementation. Here we identify a universal constraint arising from momentum conservation and the quantum nature of the preparation apparatus. Any protocol that places a particle of mass $m$ in a spatial superposition with separation $d$ necessarily entangles the particle with the center-of-mass degree of freedom of the apparatus responsible for the splitting. The resulting recoil displacement, fixed by conservation of the mass dipole moment, reduces the coherence of the particle when the apparatus is not included as part of the quantum system. For an apparatus of mass $M$, preserving coherence requires the recoil displacement to remain smaller than the coherence length of the apparatus center-of-mass state, leading to a quantitative bound expressible as a constraint on its temperature and on how rigidly the apparatus is anchored to the laboratory frame. We analyze the implications of this bound for current matter-wave interferometry experiments, proposals for gravitationally mediated entanglement, and tests of quantum mechanics near the Planck scale. Our main result, which may seem counterintuitive, is that the recoil of even heavy macroscopic apparatuses can pose a surprisingly strong constraint on the coherence of spatial superpositions of particles with masses well below the Planck mass. Finally, we discuss why the resulting limitation should be regarded as a conservative estimate and under which conditions it can be interpreted as an instance of false decoherence.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a scalable shadow-based pipeline to classify unknown quantum noise channels on 3-qubit systems. It constructs a high-dimensional feature vector from randomized Pauli measurements and employs machine learning classifiers like Random Forest to achieve reliable noise identification. This method bypasses the exponential scaling limitations of traditional tomography, offering a practical tool for near-term quantum hardware calibration.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a practical, scalable ML framework for characterizing hardware imperfections in quantum computing devices, which is vital for advancing NISQ algorithms.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Accurately classifying unknown quantum noise channels is crucial for operating near-term quantum processors, as existing methods scale exponentially with system size.

**Main result.** A random forest classifier achieved a high test accuracy of 0.8426 and macro F1 score of 0.8437, demonstrating a scalable approach to noise fingerprinting.

**Method.** The authors propose a shadow-based pipeline combining structured classical tomography with physics-informed feature engineering on randomized Pauli measurements.

**Model / system.** The work focuses on characterizing noise channels applied to simulated 3-qubit probe circuits, utilizing QAOA and simple basis states as probes.

**Key observables.** A 279-dimensional feature vector constructed from raw Pauli expectation values and derived observables from randomized measurements.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The study is designed for NISQ devices and aims to resolve distinctions between physically similar noise types.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis uses simulated Qiskit noise channels, and the current method provides discrete classification rather than continuous parameter estimation.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the four stages of the pipeline (probe preparation $ightarrow$ noisy execution $ightarrow$ shadow measurements $ightarrow$ ML classification). Table I summarizes classifier performance metrics.

**Paper structure.** The paper details a multi-stage pipeline: first, preparing probe circuits; second, executing them on a noisy simulator to gather randomized Pauli measurement data; third, constructing the high-dimensional feature vector; and finally, applying and benchmarking multiple machine learning classifiers for noise type identification.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Accurate noise classification is essential for operating near-term quantum processors, yet existing approaches, such as quantum process tomography, scale exponentially with system size, limiting their practicality for routine calibration. We propose a scalable noise fingerprinting pipeline that combines structured classical shadow tomography with physics-informed feature engineering to identify noise channels from a fixed set of 3-qubit probe circuits. Each sample is represented by a 279-dimensional feature vector constructed from randomized Pauli measurements and derived observables, designed to resolve physically similar noise channels that produce overlapping signatures under generic measurement sets. We evaluate three classifiers, i.e., random forest, extra trees, and a multilayer perceptron, on a dataset of 14,000 labeled samples spanning 10 noise types. The random forest classifier achieves the highest test accuracy of 0.8426 with a macro F1 score of 0.8437, outperforming both baselines. Confusion analysis reveals that many noise types are classified with high reliability, with the remaining confusions occurring between channels sharing similar physical decay mechanisms, motivating future work on richer probe states and noise parameter estimation.

</details>

**Summary.** This work comprehensively investigates the complex magnetic phase diagram of CrCl3, a 2D van der Waals magnet. By combining SQUID/FMR experiments with DFT+DMFT theory, the authors map out multi-stage transitions between ferromagnetic and antiferromagnetic states. The findings highlight the crucial role of persistent short-range correlations and temperature-dependent interlayer coupling in determining the material's magnetic behavior.

**Why it may be interesting.** The interplay between strong electronic correlations (as modeled by DFT+DMFT) and emergent quantum phenomena like BKT transitions in low-dimensional magnets makes this highly relevant for understanding correlated electron systems beyond simple mean-field treatments.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the evolution of spin correlations and the nature of multi-stage magnetic transitions in the quasi-two-dimensional easy-plane van der Waals magnet CrCl3.

**Main result.** The research confirms a complex magnetic phase diagram involving crossover to a spin polarized (SP) phase followed by an antiferromagnetic (AFM) ground state, while theoretical work predicts robust short-range correlations persisting well above ordering temperatures.

**Method.** The study combines experimental techniques like broadband FMR spectroscopy and DC SQUID magnetometry with advanced theoretical calculations using non-local Dynamical Mean-Field Theory (DFT+DMFT).

**Model / system.** The physical system is the van der Waals magnet CrCl3, which exhibits easy-plane XY anisotropy. The analysis focuses on the competition between intra-layer and inter-layer magnetic exchange interactions.

**Key observables.** Magnetization (M(T)), inverse susceptibility (1/χ(T)), resonant frequencies in FMR, and the magnetic correlation length ($\xi$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Transition temperatures ($T_{SP} \approx 17 	ext{ K}$, $T_N \approx 14 	ext{ K}$), Weiss temperature ($	heta$), and exchange coupling strengths ($J_1$, $J_{\perp}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The DFT calculations are noted to be performed without considering spin-orbit interaction, and the BKT formula is applied based on specific structural phases.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate SQUID magnetization curves showing magnetic transitions (cusps at 14 K), FMR intensity maps demonstrating mode evolution with temperature, and plots detailing the temperature dependence of exchange interactions and correlation lengths.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing experimental observations of multi-stage transitions using magnetometry and resonance. It then uses theoretical modeling (DFT+DMFT) to explain the persistence of short-range order and the temperature-driven crossover in interlayer coupling, culminating in a comprehensive magnetic phase diagram.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the evolution of spin correlations and the nature of the multi-stage magnetic transition in the quasi-two-dimensional easy-plane van der Waals magnet $\rm CrCl_3$. By combining broadband ferromagnetic resonance (FMR) spectroscopy and DC SQUID magnetometry on mechanically exfoliated micro-flakes with non-local dynamical mean-field theory (DFT+DMFT) calculations, we analyze both long- and short range magnetic order in CrCl$_3$. Experimentally, SQUID and FMR measurements confirm the presence of the crossover to a spin polarized phase with the subsequent transition into an antiferromagnetic ground state upon cooling, but show robust short-range correlations at temperatures far above the magnetic ordering temperatures. Theoretically, we show the existence of highly stable local magnetic moments at room temperature, with a giant room temperature lifetime $τ$ of 130--300 ps due to a wide Mott bandgap. Below room temperature, a rapid growth of the in-plane correlation length $ξ$ signals the formation of strong short range magnetic order consistent with the experimental observations. We also obtain a temperature-driven crossover of the interlayer exchange interaction, which changes from positive (ferromagnetic) at high temperatures to negative (antiferromagnetic) in the low-temperature ordered phase.

</details>

### [Hybrid Quantum and Classical Workload Management with Graph-based Scheduling](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09151v1)

**Authors:** Vanessa Sochat, Daniel Milroy

**Summary.** This work addresses the significant orchestration challenge of running hybrid quantum-classical computations in cloud environments by introducing Fluence, a Kubernetes scheduler plugin. It solves the 'two-queue problem' by treating remote QPUs as first-class resources in a scheduling graph. The results demonstrate massive improvements in efficiency, cost reduction, and time-to-result compared to standard schedulers.

**Why it may be interesting.** While highly focused on infrastructure and scheduling rather than fundamental physics, the paper's ability to manage complex resource dependencies is crucial for enabling future quantum algorithms that require tight coupling between classical optimization loops and quantum hardware execution.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is orchestrating complex hybrid quantum-classical workflows within modern HPC environments that incorporate remote Quantum Processing Units (QPUs). This introduces a 'two-queue problem' due to the external queue of the quantum device.

**Main result.** The authors present Fluence, a Kubernetes scheduler plugin that successfully manages this complexity by enabling atomic gang scheduling, synchronizing classical workers with quantum readiness, and optimizing resource selection based on cost and queue depth.

**Method.** They developed a graph-based scheduler (Fluxion) to model QPUs as first-class, attributed resources within the cloud-native Kubernetes environment. This allows for informed placement decisions that coordinate disparate computational stages.

**Model / system.** The system involves hybrid quantum-classical workloads running on platforms like AWS Braket and real QPUs, managed via a Kubernetes scheduler plugin (Fluence). The scheduling model treats classical compute nodes and remote QPU backends as interconnected resources in a resource graph.

**Key observables.** Wasted node-time from partial gang placement, worker idle time reduction (up to 5x), mean per-run cost reduction ($\sim$70x), and time-to-result improvement (hours to minutes).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Queue depth of the remote QPU backend, computational workload type (e.g., VQA loops), and resource cost metrics.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The solution assumes that quantum devices can be modeled as first-class resources within a graph structure, allowing scheduling policies to query their availability alongside classical hardware.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the 'two queue problem' concept, the hierarchical modeling of hybrid resources in the Fluxion graph, and policy selection trade-offs between minimizing cost versus minimizing queue depth.

**Paper structure.** The paper first defines the technical challenge (the two-queue problem) inherent in cloud-native quantum integration. It then introduces Fluence as the solution, detailing its mechanisms for atomic gang placement, synchronization primitives, and backend selection policies. Finally, it validates these methods across multiple real-world scenarios.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

High Performance Computing (HPC) centers are expanding to encompass resources that extend beyond traditional computing. By extending resources to quantum computing, hybrid quantum-classical workflows tackle complex optimization problems that have never before been possible. However, integrating quantum processing units (QPUs) into cloud-native and scientific workload managers presents a unique orchestration challenge: remote quantum devices introduce a second, external queue -- a two-queue problem -- alongside the queue owned by the traditional scheduler. In this work we present Fluence, a Kubernetes scheduler plugin backed by the Fluxion graph-based scheduler, that enables informed, gang-scheduled placement for quantum-classical workloads and custom resources. We evaluate Fluence across three scenarios using AWS Braket simulators and real QPUs. First, under node contention, Fluence's atomic gang placement all but eliminates the wasted node-time that a default scheduler accrues by partially placing gangs. Second, we introduce a synchronization primitive for the two-queue problem in which a single producer submits a shared quantum task while consumers remain scheduling-gated, reducing worker idle time by roughly 5x under short device queues and by orders of magnitude when a real device queue stretched to hours. Third, cost- and queue-aware backend selection pins the cheapest or shortest-queue device satisfying a workload, cutting mean per-run cost by roughly 70x and time-to-result from hours to under a minute. Together, these results show that quantum-awareness can be added to a cloud-native scheduler without modifying user containers.

</details>

**Summary.** This work theoretically analyzes the complex phase diagram of SU(N) two-leg fermionic ladders, focusing on the competition between conventional density-wave states and unconventional loop-current ordered phases. By utilizing advanced techniques like bosonization and duality symmetries, the authors demonstrate that these competing orders are mathematically linked, providing a unified framework for understanding electronic correlations in low-dimensional quantum materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** The deep connection established between exotic, time-reversal symmetry breaking loop currents and standard charge/bond density waves via exact duality symmetries provides powerful theoretical tools for understanding competing orders in strongly correlated materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates loop-current ordered phases in half-filled SU(N) two-leg fermionic ladders and elucidates the relationship between these unconventional orders and conventional density-wave states via duality symmetries.

**Main result.** The model predicts four competing orders, showing that for N>2, loop-current phases are stabilized by Hund's interaction; furthermore, an exact density-current duality symmetry relates current-carrying phases to density-wave phases.

**Method.** The analysis employs low-energy effective theories, renormalization group (RG) techniques, and bosonization methods applied to the continuum limit of the lattice Hamiltonian.

**Model / system.** The system is an SU(N) two-leg fermionic ladder described by a generalized Hubbard-type Hamiltonian incorporating hopping, on-site repulsion (U), interchain interactions (V, J_perp), and pair-hopping terms. The continuum limit yields massless Dirac fermions.

**Key observables.** Loop-current order parameters ($	ext{OAF}_\perp$, $	ext{OAF}_d$), conventional density-wave order parameters ($	ext{OCDW}^-$, $	ext{OBDW}$), and various current operators defining the symmetry algebra.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Half-filling, SU(N) structure, Hund's coupling ($J_\perp$), doping level, and interaction strengths (U, V).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on low-energy approximations, continuum limits, and the existence of specific duality symmetries ($D_{dc}$ and $D$).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the two loop-current ordered phases breaking T-symmetry, the relationship between the four competing orders via duality symmetries, and the structure of the generalized SU(N) ladder Hamiltonian.

**Paper structure.** The paper moves from defining the full lattice Hamiltonian to deriving low-energy continuum descriptions using bosonization. It then systematically analyzes the symmetry constraints imposed by density-current dualities ($D_{dc}$), leading to a classification of the current algebra ($\mathfrak{so}(4N)$) and identifying which phases are stable under RG flow.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the formation of loop-current ordered phases in half-filled SU($N$) two-leg fermionic ladders. Using a low-energy approach, we uncover the existence of non-perturbative duality symmetries relating four competing orders. Two of these orders correspond to loop-current ordered phases that spontaneously break the time-reversal symmetry and describe charge currents circulating in a staggered pattern either around the plaquettes or along the diagonals of the ladder. These unconventional phases are shown to be dual to conventional (charge and bond) density-wave phases through an exact density-current duality symmetry existing on the lattice. From a perturbative renormalization group approach, we find that these phases for $N>2$ are stabilized in a half-filled SU($N$) two-leg Hubbard ladder with an additional SU($N$) Hund's interaction. The effect of a small doping on these phases is also discussed.

</details>

### [Generalized Nonlinear Imaginary-Time Evolution](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09599v1)

**Authors:** Chenyu Shi, Hao Wang, Jin-Fu Chen  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.09599v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **1/5** · `correlated cavity matter` **1/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **1/5** · `entanglement & information structure` **1/5** · `non-equilibrium dynamics` **1/5**

<div class="figure-strip-hint">↔ Scroll figures horizontally</div>

<img src="2607.09599_figures/2607.09599_fig1.jpg" alt="Figure 1">
<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 1.</b> FIG. 1. Illustrative diagram comparing ITE and NITE. Stan- dard ITE induces an energy-descent flow on the accessible state manifold, driving any initial state with nonzero ground- state overlap toward the ground-state subspace. The up- date direction is given by the tangent-space projection of −H |ϕ(t)⟩. NITE generalizes this picture to state-dependent effective generators associated with general cost functions. Its cost landscape may contain multiple local minima, and the re- sulting flow can converge to different solutions depending on the initialization. The NITE update direction is also given by the tangent-space projection of −H(ϕ(t)) |ϕ(t)⟩.</figcaption>
</figure>
<figure class="paper-figure-card">
<img src="2607.09599_figures/2607.09599_fig2.jpg" alt="Figure 2">
<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 2.</b> FIG. 2. Comparison of NITE and standard gradient descent learning curves in three subroutine tasks. (a) Variance min- imization. (b) QFI maximization, with the red dash-dotted line indicating the theoretical QFI upper bound. (c,d) Simul- taneous preparation of the ground state and first excited state, where the two panels show the energies of the prepared states and the red dash-dotted lines indicate the corresponding ex- act eigen energies. Solid blue curves denote NITE, dashed orange curves denote GD, and shaded regions show one stan- dard deviation over 10 independent runs.</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** The paper introduces Nonlinear Imaginary-Time Evolution (NITE) as a generalization of ITE to optimize any quantum cost function, not just energy. It rigorously proves that NITE converges exponentially fast by utilizing the geometry of the state space manifold. This makes it a versatile and theoretically robust optimization method for modern variational quantum algorithms.

**Why it may be interesting.** This provides a powerful, geometrically grounded optimization tool that generalizes standard techniques like imaginary-time evolution for state preparation in NISQ algorithms.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The work generalizes standard Imaginary-Time Evolution (ITE) from solely finding ground states to optimizing arbitrary quantum state preparation tasks.

**Main result.** A Nonlinear Imaginary-Time Evolution (NITE) framework is proposed, which achieves local exponential convergence rates for various variational optimization problems beyond simple energy minimization.

**Method.** NITE defines the evolution as a gradient flow using the Fubini–Study metric applied to a general cost function $E(\phi)$, leading to state-dependent effective generators.

**Model / system.** The system involves pure quantum states $|\phiangle$ parameterized by variational circuits, allowing for optimization of multi-state systems and complex objectives like variance minimization or QFI maximization.

**Key observables.** Energy expectation value $\langle H angle$, general cost function $E(\phi)$, Quantum Fisher Information (QFI), and the convergence rate ($\mu$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The local minimum must be non-degenerate, and the analysis relies on establishing strong geodesic convexity near the target state.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Convergence proofs assume the cost function is sufficiently smooth ($C^2$) and that a small neighborhood around the optimum exhibits strong geodesic convexity (Polyak–Łojasiewicz inequality).

**Figures summary.** Figures compare NITE learning curves against standard Gradient Descent (GD) across tasks like variance minimization and QFI maximization, consistently showing NITE's superior linear convergence rate.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the generalization from ITE to NITE for arbitrary cost functions. It then details the mathematical formulation using Fubini-Study geometry, proves the local exponential convergence rate, and applies this framework to several practical variational tasks (e.g., VQE, quantum sensing).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Imaginary-time evolution (ITE) is a powerful method for ground-state preparation of a given Hamiltonian. The normalized ITE can be viewed as a gradient flow of the energy expectation value with respect to the Fubini--Study metric. In this work, we propose a generalized nonlinear imaginary-time evolution (NITE) for more general quantum state-preparation tasks. We further present a hardware-efficient variational implementation of NITE and reveal its connection to quantum natural gradient descent. NITE is applied to several subroutine tasks, including variance minimization in variational eigensolvers, probe-state preparation in variational quantum sensing, and excited-state preparation using penalty terms. We prove that NITE achieves a local exponential convergence rate under reasonable assumptions. Our results show that NITE outperforms standard gradient descent and can serve as an efficient optimization method for variational tasks beyond ground-state preparation.

</details>

**Authors:** Brennan Bell, Inti Gabriel Mendoza Estrada, Andreas Trügler, Paul Erker  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.09378v1>

**Summary.** This paper models quantum repeater network routing as an adversarial game where an attacker (Eve) tries to degrade entanglement links while a sender (Alice) chooses routes. By using advanced co-learning algorithms and analyzing the resulting strategies, the authors demonstrate that network bottlenecks are critical vulnerabilities, providing both quantitative robustness measures and explainable insights into these weaknesses.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous framework for testing the resilience of quantum communication protocols against sophisticated, adaptive adversaries, which is crucial for designing practical quantum internet infrastructure.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates adversarial robustness in quantum-network routing when resources (routes) are limited, framed as an adversarial bandit problem.

**Main result.** Adversarial co-learning successfully recovers the minimax strategy structure, showing that network bottlenecks lead to zero retention while non-bottleneck paths follow a predictable $1 - 1/N$ coverage principle.

**Method.** The authors employ adversarial co-learning (Exp3) against simulated quantum communication transcripts and use decision-tree regressors followed by LLMs for explainability analysis.

**Model / system.** The model is an entanglement-based quantum repeater network utilizing the Ekert-91 (E91) protocol. The game involves Alice selecting a route and Eve choosing an attack surface (edge intercept–resend or memory degradation).

**Key observables.** CHSH violation ($|S|$), learned retention rate, Nash equilibrium gap decay, and decision-tree faithfulness ($R^2$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** 50 structured topologies across eight structural families; the CHSH bound ($	ext{S} > 2$); finite sample size for learning.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that learned strategies can approximate the full-matrix minimax reference, and that LLMs can be prompted to summarize complex decision evidence.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate exemplary topologies (bottleneck vs. disjoint), compare theoretical oracle retention ($1 - 1/N$) against observed learning results, and show probability mass distributions of learned strategies for Alice and Eve.

**Paper structure.** The paper first sets up the adversarial bandit game on quantum repeaters using E91 transcripts. It then applies adversarial co-learning to measure robustness across various topologies. Finally, it develops an open-source explanation workflow by fitting decision trees to map topology features to strategic outcomes and summarizing these findings with LLMs.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study an adversarial bandit problem for entanglement-based quantum-network routing over a modest graph corpus. Alice selects an end-to-end repeater route for an Ekert-91 protocol (E91) representing her move, while Eve selects an attack surface, either edge intercept--resend or repeater memory degradation. Payoffs are drawn from cached SeQUeNCe-simulated E91 transcripts, and Alice accepts a turn when the finite-sample statistic violates the Clauser-Horne-Shimony-Holt (CHSH) bound. Performing adversarial co-learning across 50 structured topologies, we find that learned retention tracks a full-matrix minimax reference closely (Pearson $r=0.99$): under a one-surface Eve action model, bottleneck families have zero retention, while non-bottleneck families follow a $1-1/N$ coverage principle. We then fit decision-tree explanation models to graph-, attack-, and route-level topology-corpus targets and report their faithfulness. Finally, we construct prompt records for local language models to summarize the tree evidence, resulting in an open-source explanation workflow for quantum-repeater network games.

</details>

**Authors:** Abhishek Mishra, Moisés Bermejo Morán, Stefano Pironio  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.08841v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5** · `analog quantum simulation` **1/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **1/5** · `entanglement & information structure` **1/5**

**Summary.** This paper develops Partially-Commutative Polynomial Optimization (PCPO), a generalization for optimizing polynomials whose variables only partially commute. It achieves this by constructing compact Semidefinite Programming relaxations that natively encode these partial commutation relations into the monomial structure, offering a significant computational improvement over methods relying solely on linear constraints.

**Why it may be interesting.** For theorists working on open quantum systems or quantum information, this provides a powerful mathematical tool to analyze complex physical models (like multi-party entanglement measures) where operators do not commute fully but obey specific partial commutation rules.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses Partially-Commutative Polynomial Optimization (PCPO), a generalization of standard commutative and non-commutative optimization problems.

**Main result.** It introduces PCPO, which models systems where variables exhibit partial commutation relations, and shows that these can be incorporated into compact Semidefinite Programming (SDP) relaxations by exploiting the underlying partially-commutative monoid structure.

**Method.** The core method involves using SDP hierarchies derived from moment matrix theory, but crucially modifies the basis construction to natively implement partial commutation relations algebraically rather than through simple linear constraints.

**Model / system.** The framework is motivated by quantum information scenarios involving multiple parties acting on shared systems, where operators exhibit selective commutation (partial commutations). This interpolates between fully commutative and fully non-commutative regimes.

**Key observables.** Optimization of polynomials over partially-commuting variables; the objective value $\langle\psi, p(A)\psiangle$ in the tracial setting.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The degree $d$ of the SDP relaxation hierarchy; the specific set of partial commutation relations defining the monoid structure.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The approach assumes that the algebraic properties derived from the partially-commutative monoid can be used to construct a reduced monomial basis for the SDP, avoiding the computational difficulties associated with general quotient algebra representations.

**Paper structure.** The paper reviews standard Non-Commutative Polynomial Optimization (NCPO) using NPA hierarchies before introducing PCPO. It focuses on adapting techniques from partially-commutative monoids to build efficient, constraint-free SDP relaxations for the general case.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Semidefinite programming hierarchies for commutative and non-commutative polynomial optimization represent a powerful computational tool with many applications in quantum information. In such applications, a given variable is typically not either commuting or non-commuting with all other variables, but instead commutes with some variables and does not commute with others, i.e., the variables satisfy some partial commutation relations. While such partial commutation relations can always be incorporated in a fully non-commutative setting through suitable linear constraints in the semidefinite programming relaxations, exploiting their algebraic properties from the onset can result in more compact relaxations. This leads us to introduce partially-commutative polynomial optimization, a framework that encompasses commutative and non-commutative polynomial optimization, allowing for arbitrary commutation relations among the variables. We point out that the underlying algebraic structure is that of a partially-commutative monoid. We present and review several key aspects of such monoids and show how they can be used to build SDP relaxations for partially-commutative polynomial optimization problems in which the partial commutations are natively implemented in the monomial structure, without the need of additional linear constraints.

</details>

### [COSMA: Communication-aware Optimization of Fermionic Simulation Kernels for Modular Quantum Architectures](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09381v1)

**Authors:** Enrico Russo, Francesco G. Blanco, Elio Vinciguerra, Davide Patti, Giuseppe Ascia, Maurizio Palesi  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.09381v1>

**Summary.** This paper introduces COSMA, a novel compilation framework designed to efficiently run fermionic simulations on multi-core quantum hardware. It tackles the critical bottleneck of inter-core communication by jointly optimizing the mapping from fermions to qubits, the scheduling of Pauli operations, and the physical qubit layout. The results show significant reductions in communication overhead compared to existing methods.

**Why it may be interesting.** It directly addresses the practical limitations of scaling quantum algorithms by providing a systematic compiler approach to manage physical connectivity constraints inherent in modular quantum processors.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Scaling quantum simulation for chemically relevant problems requires modular architectures, but inter-core quantum communication becomes a major performance bottleneck.

**Main result.** COSMA achieves up to 2.5x reduction in communication cost compared to state-of-the-art baselines, demonstrating that cross-layer co-design is essential for scalable simulation on multi-core hardware.

**Method.** The framework jointly optimizes fermion-to-qubit mapping, Pauli scheduling, and qubit allocation to minimize inter-core state transfers across modular quantum units.

**Model / system.** The system involves simulating fermionic dynamics governed by a Hamiltonian decomposed into Pauli strings, which are then mapped onto qubits for execution on interconnected Quantum Processing Units (QPUs).

**Key observables.** Inter-core communication cost (the primary metric minimized), circuit depth, and simulation accuracy.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of fermionic modes, the size/connectivity of the modular architecture, and the Trotterization step size.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The approach assumes a modular quantum hardware structure where inter-core communication cost is the dominant performance limiter; it also uses approximations like the first-order Trotter formula.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the compilation flow (Mapping -> Scheduling -> Synthesis -> Allocation) and provide examples of specific mappings (e.g., JKMN-like ternary trees) and circuit syntheses for a single Trotter step.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the problem formulation, introduces COSMA as a cross-layer co-design framework, describes the joint optimization process involving mapping choices, Pauli scheduling, and hardware layout, and validates results on molecular benchmarks.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum simulation is a leading application of quantum computing, but scaling to chemically relevant problems requires modular architectures composed of interconnected quantum processing units. In such systems, inter-core quantum communication becomes a major performance bottleneck. In this work, we present COSMA, a communication-aware compilation framework for fermionic simulation kernels targeting modular quantum architectures. Our approach jointly optimizes fermion-to-qubit mapping, Pauli scheduling, and qubit allocation to minimize inter-core state transfers. Evaluated on molecular benchmarks, COSMA achieves up to $2.5\times$ reduction in communication cost compared to state-of-the-art baselines, with a median improvement of $1.7\times$. These results demonstrate that cross-layer co-design is essential for efficient and scalable quantum simulation on multi-core quantum hardware.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper presents a scalable, two-part diagnostic framework—ORS and $	ext{Reff}$—to characterize quantum reservoirs used in QML. It overcomes the exponential scaling problem of older diagnostics by focusing only on top output probabilities and input feature rank. The results confirm that high expressivity (near Haar randomness) combined with sufficient feature coverage leads to superior machine learning performance.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a crucial set of metrics for assessing the hardware readiness and theoretical limits of variational quantum algorithms by decoupling intrinsic expressivity from task-specific feature utilization.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Existing methods for diagnosing quantum reservoir expressivity scale exponentially with system size, making them impractical for large-scale hardware.

**Main result.** The authors introduce a scalable framework using two complementary diagnostics—an Order-Statistics (ORS) score and an Effective Rank ($	ext{Reff}$)—that successfully correlates intrinsic expressivity with usable predictive information on quantum machine learning tasks.

**Method.** They propose the ORS score, which analyzes only the top output probabilities against a Haar baseline, and $	ext{Reff}$, which measures input-dependent feature utility. These are combined to provide hardware-agnostic diagnostics.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on Quantum Reservoirs (QRs) used in Quantum Reservoir Computing (QRC) and Quantum Extreme Learning Machines (QELMs). The reservoirs are constructed using various fixed random quantum circuits (e.g., G1, G2, G3 families) tested across synthetic tasks and real-world benchmarks like LiH chemistry regression.

**Key observables.** Order-Statistics (ORS) gap ($\Lambda - \Lambda_{	ext{Haar}}$), Effective Rank ($	ext{Reff}$), Test Mean Squared Error (MSE), and $R^2$ correlation coefficients.

**Important parameters / regimes.** System size/Hilbert-space dimension ($D$), effective fidelity ($f_{Bq}$), number of gates, and the choice between commuting vs. non-commuting circuit constructions.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The noise correction assumes a dominant global depolarizing channel; the analytical derivations for ORS rely on the large-$D$ approximation.

**Figures summary.** Figures demonstrate that reservoir families approaching Haar randomness (like G3) show high ORS scores and $	ext{Reff}$, correlating with low test MSE. The comparison between different circuit types shows how non-commuting structures maintain better performance.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the scalability problem, then details the two diagnostics (ORS and $	ext{Reff}$), validates them against established theory and noise models, and finally applies the combined framework to benchmark real-world quantum machine learning tasks.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum reservoirs offer a hardware-friendly route to quantum machine learning, replacing trainable circuits with fixed random dynamics and a classical readout. Because the reservoir is not optimized, performance depends entirely on the choice of reservoir family, yet existing diagnostics demand resources that grow exponentially with system size. We introduce a scalable, hardware-agnostic framework built on two complementary quantities. The first is a task-independent order-statistics (ORS) expressivity score, which compares only the largest output probabilities of a reservoir ensemble against an analytical Haar baseline. It never reconstructs the full output distribution, is cost-independent of Hilbert-space dimension, and admits a closed-form depolarizing noise correction, making it directly usable on hardware. The second is the task-dependent effective rank $R_{\mathrm{eff}}$ of the feature matrix, which measures how much input-dependent information reaches the readout. We validate the ORS score against established complexity diagnostics and confirm it remains informative under simulated noise and on IBM quantum hardware. Across synthetic and real quantum extreme learning machine and quantum reservoir computing benchmarks, ORS captures the intrinsic expressivity hierarchy of reservoir families while $R_{\mathrm{eff}}$ determines when that expressivity becomes usable predictive information.

</details>

### [Quantum-Enhanced Synthetic Data Generation Using Quantum Circuit Born Machines for Imbalanced Tabular Learning](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09113v1)

**Authors:** Tanapol Nuatho, Narisorn Sangnakara, Prapong Prechaprapranwong, Rajchawit Sarochawikasit  
**Type:** both · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.09113v1>

**Summary.** This work proposes using a Quantum Circuit Born Machine (QCBM) within a hybrid quantum-classical pipeline to generate synthetic data for imbalanced tabular datasets. By leveraging quantum properties like entanglement, the QCBM achieves superior structural similarity compared to classical oversampling methods. This augmentation significantly boosts downstream classification performance on real-world benchmarks.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on ML, the paper's reliance on variational quantum circuits to model complex probability distributions touches upon quantum state tomography and characterizing quantum process fidelity, which are relevant areas in quantum information theory.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses data scarcity and class imbalance, common challenges in machine learning that degrade model generalization.

**Main result.** Using a Quantum Circuit Born Machine (QCBM) for synthetic data augmentation improves classification metrics like F1-score by 5–15% on structured tabular datasets.

**Method.** A hybrid quantum-classical framework is employed where the QCBM models complex probability distributions to generate synthetic samples, which are then used to augment imbalanced training data.

**Model / system.** The core model is the Quantum Circuit Born Machine (QCBM), which utilizes a parameterized variational quantum circuit $U(\theta)$ exploiting superposition and entanglement. The process involves encoding classical data into quantum states for distribution modeling.

**Key observables.** Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence, Maximum Mean Discrepancy (MMD), F1-score, and minority-class recall are used to quantify distribution fidelity and classification performance.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The synthetic data augmentation ratio (tested at 30–50% of the minority class), circuit depth ($L$), and optimization parameters ($	heta$) are key variables.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Assumptions include the tractability of basis encoding via PCA dimensionality reduction for near-term quantum simulators, and that the QCBM can capture complex cross-feature correlations better than classical methods like SMOTE.

**Figures summary.** The notes mention tables comparing KL Divergence and MMD across datasets (Iris/Telco) and performance metrics (F1, AUC-ROC) for various oversampling techniques.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem, details the QCBM framework (preprocessing via PCA, training by minimizing KL divergence using parameter-shift rule), evaluates its efficacy through data augmentation protocols (DA, TSTR, TRTS), and compares results against established classical baselines.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Data scarcity and class imbalance are persistent challenges in machine learning that degrade model generalization and introduce predictive bias. We present a hybrid quantum-classical framework for synthetic data generation using a Quantum Circuit Born Machine (QCBM) to address these limitations. The proposed approach exploits quantum mechanical properties -- superposition and entanglement -- within a parameterized variational quantum circuit to model complex probability distributions that are difficult for classical generative methods to capture. Experiments are conducted on two tabular benchmark datasets: the Iris dataset and the Telco Customer Churn dataset. Preprocessing includes normalization and PCA-based dimensionality reduction to enable efficient basis encoding for quantum circuits. The QCBM is trained by minimizing Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence between real and generated data distributions using a gradient-based parameter-shift optimization rule. Augmenting training data with QCBM-generated synthetic samples at 40-50% of the minority class improves F1-score by approximately 5-15% and minority-class recall by 10-25%. Cross-domain evaluations (Train on Synthetic, Test on Real; and Train on Real, Test on Synthetic) reveal a performance gap of only 3-10%, indicating strong distributional fidelity. Comparative analysis against classical oversampling methods -- SMOTE, Borderline-SMOTE, KMeansSMOTE, and SVM-SMOTE -- shows that QCBM achieves competitive classification performance and produces lower Maximum Mean Discrepancy (MMD) on the Telco dataset, suggesting superior structural similarity in certain imbalanced settings. These findings establish QCBM as a viable complementary tool for data augmentation, particularly for low-dimensional structured tabular data with class imbalance.

</details>
