**Summary.** This work details a systematic method for adapting quantum computation techniques to solve Partial Differential Equations. It establishes a clear 'quantum path' by connecting classical numerical discretizations to fundamental quantum operations like Hamiltonian simulation and QSVT via block encoding. The paper is valuable as a shared vocabulary, aiming to guide researchers from both PDE theory and quantum computing into this application space.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous framework for applying advanced quantum algorithms to model complex dynamics described by PDEs, which is relevant for understanding open quantum systems or non-equilibrium processes where continuum descriptions are necessary.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is developing quantum algorithms capable of solving Partial Differential Equations (PDEs), which bridges classical numerical analysis with quantum computation.

**Main result.** The paper outlines a comprehensive, mathematically transparent pipeline showing how continuous PDEs can be mapped to quantum circuits using block encoding and various quantum primitives.

**Method.** It employs a structured approach starting from standard finite difference/element discretizations, embedding the resulting operators into unitaries, and then applying techniques like QSVT and Hamiltonian simulation.

**Model / system.** The focus is on general PDEs (elliptic, hyperbolic, parabolic, and nonlinear), treating them as computational problems rather than physical Hamiltonians in a standard quantum simulation sense. The system is modeled via its discretized differential operator.

**Key observables.** The extraction of a quantity of interest from the PDE solution, alongside diagnostics like discretization error, state preparation cost, and measurement feasibility.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Discretization error, normalization factors, and computational costs associated with various quantum primitives (e.g., QSVT depth).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The approach assumes that the problem can be successfully mapped to a unitary evolution framework via block encoding, though it notes limitations regarding universal speedup.

**Paper structure.** The structure follows the full computational pipeline: starting with classical discretization (finite difference/element) $ightarrow$ quantum encoding $ightarrow$ transformation using quantum primitives (QSVT, Hamiltonian simulation) $ightarrow$ extraction of the desired quantity.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Partial differential equations are a promising application area for fault-tolerant quantum algorithms, but the subject lies between two communities with different languages: numerical analysis and quantum computation. These lecture notes provide a numerically grounded introduction for readers entering from either field. Block encoding is the organizing principle: once a discretized differential operator is embedded in a unitary, primitives such as quantum singular value transformation, Hamiltonian simulation, linear combinations of unitaries, amplitude amplification, and measurement can be assembled into algorithms for elliptic, hyperbolic, and parabolic PDEs. Each chapter begins with a standard finite difference or finite element discretization and follows the full pipeline from the continuous PDE to quantum encoding, transformation, and extraction of a quantity of interest. Particular attention is paid to the factors governing end-to-end performance, including discretization error, state preparation, normalization, postselection, and measurement cost. A final chapter introduces nonlinear problems through Carleman and Koopman-von Neumann linearizations. The aim is not a comprehensive survey or a claim of universal quantum advantage, but a mathematically transparent entry point and a shared vocabulary for researchers in both communities.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper tackles the theoretical problem of covert communication—sending a message secretly—over quantum Multiple Access Channels (MACs). The authors establish positive achievable rates for both fully quantum and mixed classical-quantum MAC settings. By developing rigorous rate region calculations, they provide fundamental limits on secure information transfer in these complex multi-user quantum environments.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work pushes the boundaries of quantum security protocols by quantifying how much classical information can be reliably transmitted while remaining undetectable to an eavesdropper in complex multi-user quantum channels.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study focuses on achieving keyless covert classical communication over quantum Multiple-Access Channels (MACs) when the message sets are general.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate the feasibility of positive covert rates over both fully quantum and classical-quantum MACs, establishing general one-shot and asymptotic achievable rate regions for the first time in this combined setting.

**Method.** The analysis employs advanced information theoretic tools, including random codebook generation, superposition coding, simultaneous pinching, and channel resolvability to establish upper and lower bounds on the covert rate.

**Model / system.** The system models include fully quantum MACs and classical-quantum MACs with an arbitrary number of transmitters. Specific examples analyzed are a finite-dimensional MAC with a helper, a classical Gaussian MAC, and a single-mode bosonic MAC.

**Key observables.** Covert rate, covert capacity, achievable rate region, Fidelity Distance, Purified Distance, Quantum Relative Entropy.

**Important parameters / regimes.** One-shot vs. asymptotic regimes ($n 	o \infty$), the size/nature of the message sets (general), and error probability bounds ($\delta$, $\epsilon$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis builds upon established techniques like simultaneous pinching and relies on extending previous work concerning covert communication over quantum MACs.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the general setup where multiple transmitters cooperate to communicate covertly with a legitimate receiver while evading an adversary (warden).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by defining the problem in terms of state distances and relative entropies, establishing achievability via coding schemes, deriving upper bounds using information inequalities related to ancestral/descendant sub-graphs, and finally synthesizing these results into concrete rate regions.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study covert classical communication over quantum multiple-access channels (MACs) with general message sets. Specifically, we consider a fully quantum MAC with arbitrary message sets and an arbitrary number of transmitters. We demonstrate the feasibility of achieving a positive covert rate over this channel and establish general one-shot and asymptotic achievable rate regions. For classical-quantum MACs with general message sets, we establish the covert capacity, when the transmitters are restricted to deterministic encoding. Our result recovers, as a special case, known results for classical communication over classical MACs with general message sets, covert communication of a classical message over a classical channel with two transmitters, and classical communication over quantum MACs. We provide three examples of MACs to which our results can be applied, either directly or indirectly, to achieve positive covert rates. Specifically, we first study covert communication over a finite-dimensional MAC with a helper. We then analyze a classical Gaussian MAC with a helper and derive its covert capacity. Finally, we extend the analysis to a single-mode bosonic MAC with a helper and show that positive covert rates can also be achieved in this setting. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first work to achieve positive-rate covert communication over both classical and quantum MACs.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper proposes an advanced $	ext{Si}/	ext{SiGe}$ heterostructure design—combining a narrow well, pure-Ge cap, and Ge spike—to drastically enhance valley splitting in spin qubit platforms. Using detailed simulations, the authors demonstrate that this engineered structure can achieve splittings up to $5 	ext{ meV}$, effectively mitigating valley splitting as an obstacle for scalable quantum processors.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses a critical material science bottleneck for solid-state quantum computing by providing an engineering blueprint (heterostructure design) to overcome fundamental energy level limitations in silicon qubits.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is achieving valley splittings significantly larger than thermal energy ($k_B T$) in Si/SiGe heterostructures to ensure reliable initialization, operation, and readout of spin qubits.

**Main result.** An unorthodox design combining a narrow quantum well, a pure-Ge cap, and a dilute Ge spike can push valley splittings up to $\sim 5 	ext{ meV}$ with low variance across disorder realizations.

**Method.** The work uses device-scale simulations incorporating atomistic alloy disorder via 1D tight-binding theory, corroborated by targeted DFT calculations.

**Model / system.** The system is gate-defined Si/SiGe heterostructures designed for spin qubits. The model involves engineering the Ge concentration profile ($\chi(z)$) through a specific three-part structure: narrow quantum well, pure-Ge cap, and dilute Ge spike.

**Key observables.** Valley splitting ($\Delta E_v$), which must be significantly larger than $k_B T$ (i.e., $> 1 	ext{ meV}$ for reliable operation).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Well width ($W_w$, e.g., $2.5-3.0 	ext{ nm}$), pure-Ge cap thickness ($	ext{NML}=1$), and dilute Ge spike concentration ($f_{	ext{Ge}} \approx 4\%$). Interface sharpness ($\sigma$) is critical.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes atomically sharp interfaces to establish an upper bound, though it quantifies the required interface sharpness (e.g., $\sigma_{cap} \approx 1-2 	ext{ ML}$) needed for $E_v \gtrsim 1 	ext{ meV}$ in realistic scenarios.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate how valley splitting varies with well width, cap thickness, and spike concentration, demonstrating the additive enhancement from the proposed three-component structure. Later figures quantify the dependence on interface broadening ($\sigma_{cap}, \sigma_{spike}$).

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical requirement for large valley splittings, proposes an unorthodox $	ext{Si}/	ext{SiGe}$ design (narrow well + pure-Ge cap + Ge spike), and uses 1D tight-binding/DFT simulations to show that this combination yields high, reproducible splitting values. It then analyzes how interface broadening degrades this performance.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Achieving valley splittings well in excess of the thermal energy of electrons and avoiding valley excitations is essential for the consistent initialization, operation and readout of gate-defined Si spin qubits. In this work, we present a device-level optimization strategy for pushing valley splittings to between 1 and 5 meV, well beyond values reported in nearly all previous theoretical studies. Using device-scale simulations that incorporate atomistic alloy disorder through a 1D tight-binding theory, we demonstrate that our proposed approach yields large valley splittings with a tight distribution across disorder realizations, a key requirement for reproducible qubit performance at scale. The approach rests on an unorthodox Si/SiGe heterostructure design combining a narrow quantum well, a small Ge spike, and a pure-Ge cap. We corroborate these predictions with targeted atomistic density functional theory calculations. These results offer a clear path forward for scalable Si/SiGe spin qubit devices and, if realized experimentally, effectively eliminate valley splitting as an existential problem for large scale SiGe-based quantum processors.

</details>

**Summary.** This work introduces the Stochastic Similarity Renormalization Group (SRGQMC) method, which uses Quantum Monte Carlo techniques to simulate continuous unitary transformations in nuclear physics. It successfully bypasses the prohibitive computational cost of high-order In-Medium SRG calculations by sampling operator flow stochastically. This allows for reliable predictions up to four-body correlations, significantly advancing our understanding of strongly interacting quantum matter.

**Why it may be interesting.** The stochastic approach provides a powerful computational tool for simulating complex many-body quantum dynamics where deterministic tensor contractions become intractable due to exponential scaling.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing a scalable method for high-order In-Medium Similarity Renormalization Group (IMSRG) calculations that overcomes the combinatorial tensor-space explosion associated with traditional deterministic flow equations.

**Main result.** The stochastic framework successfully achieves the first IMSRG(4) calculation, demonstrating significant improvement in convergence toward the Full Configuration Interaction (FCI) limit compared to lower-order truncations.

**Method.** It introduces Stochastic Similarity Renormalization Group with Quantum Monte Carlo (SRGQMC), which maps continuous unitary flow equations onto an ensemble of signed random walkers to sample commutator structures instead of evolving full operator matrices.

**Model / system.** The calculations are applied to nuclear many-body systems, using the Richardson pairing model for testing. The underlying physics involves evolving Hamiltonians via a continuous unitary transformation governed by flow equations up to four-body operators.

**Key observables.** Correlation energy ($E_{	ext{corr}}$), free-space scattering phase shifts, and comparison of calculated values against deterministic IMSRG orders ($	ext{IMSRG}(2)$ through $	ext{IMSRG}(4)$) and the exact FCI limit.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Coupling strength ($g$), flow parameter ($s$), walker population ($N_w$), and number of stochastic loops ($N_{	ext{loop}}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that mapping the deterministic flow onto a stochastic ensemble provides an unbiased estimate, which is confirmed by observing convergence as $N_w$ or $N_{	ext{loop}}$ increases.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare SRG/SRGQMC evolved NN interaction matrix elements and phase shifts; another compares correlation energy vs. pairing strength for different IMSRG orders; a third shows the evolution of $E_{	ext{corr}}(s)$ tracking deterministic calculations.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the need to overcome tensor-space explosion in high-order IMSRG. It then details the SRGQMC formalism, applying it first to free-space NN interactions and subsequently to the pairing model, culminating in the demonstration of $	ext{IMSRG}(4)$ feasibility.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

By integrating the quantum Monte Carlo technique into the similarity renormalization group (SRG), we have developed a stochastic SRG framework (SRGQMC) capable of both free-space two-body and in-medium many-body evolutions. This approach circumvents the combinatorial tensor-space explosion of many-body flow equations by mapping continuous unitary transformations onto an ensemble of signed random walkers. We benchmark the SRGQMC against deterministic free-space SRG evolutions of realistic nucleon-nucleon (NN) interactions, as well as against in-medium SRG (IMSRG) many-body calculations with the Richardson pairing model at two- and three-body levels [IMSRG(2)/(3)]. While a deterministic extension to the four-body level [IMSRG(4)] remains unfeasible due to prohibitive computational costs, we have achieved the first IMSRG(4) calculation by using the stochastic technique, demonstrating a substantial improvement toward the full configuration-interaction limit. This stochastic framework provides a practical pathway to higher-order IMSRG calculations.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes quasicrystals to show that increasing rotational symmetry ($N$) drives the system toward apparent randomness. The authors identify two key length scales—a crossover scale ($\kappa_N$) and a statistical unit cell size ($\lambda_N$)—which quantify how deterministic order is progressively concealed by disorder-like fluctuations.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of an emergent 'statistical unit cell' provides a powerful mathematical tool to quantify how deterministic structure persists even when conventional periodicity is absent, which has implications for understanding glassy or disordered quantum phases.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how deterministic order, characteristic of quasicrystals, can become statistically indistinguishable from true randomness as the rotational symmetry ($N$) increases.

**Main result.** Increasing $N$ causes a crossover length ($\kappa_N$) below which local statistical signatures are suppressed, leading to a geometric critical point where order and randomness merge over any finite observation window.

**Method.** The authors analyze density fluctuations ($\sigma^2(R)/R$) and pair correlation functions ($g(R)$) using generalized dual methods applied to $N$-fold quasicrystals in 1D and 2D.

**Model / system.** The system under study is perfect quasicrystals, which possess long-range order but lack translational periodicity. The analysis compares these structures against conventional crystals and amorphous matter by varying the rotational symmetry parameter $N$.

**Key observables.** Number variance ($\sigma^2(R)$), pair correlation function ($g(R)$), crossover length ($\kappa_N$), and statistical unit cell size ($\lambda_N$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Rotational symmetry $N$ (or $\epsilon = 1/N$), which controls the degree of quasiperiodicity; dimensionality (1D vs. 2D).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the statistical properties can be characterized by emergent length scales ($\kappa_N, \lambda_N$) derived from fluctuation measurements.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate density fluctuations showing crossovers near $\kappa_N$ and oscillations at $\lambda_N$. Comparisons are made with random local phasonic flips, and scaling behavior of these characteristic lengths with $N$ is quantified.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes a quantitative framework by analyzing how increasing rotational symmetry ($N$) suppresses quasiperiodic signatures. It identifies two key scales ($\kappa_N$ and $\lambda_N$), demonstrating that the system approaches a critical point where order vanishes statistically, while defining the statistical unit cell as the remaining signature of determinism.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The classical dichotomy between crystalline order and amorphous disorder is increasingly challenged by novel states that lack conventional crystalline symmetries while retaining crystal-like properties. Quasicrystals occupy a distinctive position within this expanding framework by possessing long-range order without translational periodicity, thereby permitting arbitrary $N$-fold rotational symmetry. Paradoxically, far from their unique symmetry center, high-symmetry quasicrystals closely resemble disordered patterns, raising the question of how deterministic order can be detected. Here we show that increasing rotational symmetry progressively suppresses local statistical signatures of quasiperiodicity, while preserving its underlying exact long-range order. This order is thus concealed below an emergent crossover length that grows linearly with $N$. Therefore, as $N \rightarrow \infty$, the disorder-like regime expands without bound, defining a symmetry-controlled geometric critical point at which deterministic order and randomness become statistically indistinguishable over any finite observation window. For finite $N$, however, quasiperiodic order becomes detectable beyond this crossover, revealing a second emergent length scale that we identify as the size of a \textit{statistical unit cell} -- finite patches over which statistical properties recur despite the absence of conventional translational periodicity. In one dimension, the statistical-unit-cell size coincides with the crossover length, whereas in two dimensions it grows as $N^2$, remaining smaller than the size of typical approximants and establishing a hierarchy of emergent length scales. Together, the disorder-to-order crossover and statistical unit cells provide a quantitative framework connecting crystals, quasicrystals, and amorphous matter, showing how apparent disorder can emerge from purely deterministic geometry.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces a novel parallel architecture for Quantum Convolutional Neural Networks (QCNNs) designed to overcome the exponential memory scaling problem when simulating large qubit systems. By partitioning input images and combining results through a binary reduction tree structure, the authors enable training models up to 128 qubits on classical hardware. This demonstrates that scalable quantum machine learning is possible without prohibitive computational resources.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses resource limitations in quantum machine learning by proposing a scalable simulation technique for complex quantum circuits, which is highly relevant for developing practical quantum algorithms.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is implementing Quantum Convolutional Neural Networks (QCNNs) for image classification on large datasets like MNIST while circumventing the exponential memory scaling limitations of classical simulation.

**Main result.** A novel hierarchical partitioning and binary reduction architecture allows simulating QCNNs with up to 128 qubits, achieving competitive accuracy comparable to smaller, single-process models.

**Method.** The method employs a parallelization strategy where the input image is partitioned into independent sub-images. These partitions are processed in parallel and then combined using a binary reduction tree structure involving partial tracing over density matrices.

**Model / system.** The model is a QCNN built upon parameterized quantum circuits, utilizing Z-feature maps for encoding pixel data. The architecture mimics classical CNNs by alternating convolutional layers (using 2-qubit unitaries) and pooling layers that condense information via partial tracing.

**Key observables.** Binary classification accuracy on the MNIST dataset, measured by the Pauli Z expectation value ($\langle \sigma_Z angle$) of the final qubit.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The model was tested up to 128 qubits; parameter counting formulas were derived for convolutional and reduction blocks.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The simulation relies on approximating the full state vector using density matrices ($ho$) and assuming that pooling layers allow for discarding irrelevant information via partial tracing. The process requires the number of processes to be a power of two.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the QCNN structure, showing how an image is partitioned (e.g., 128 qubits split into smaller blocks) and combined through a binary reduction tree, with comparisons made between single-process vs. parallel accuracy results for various qubit counts.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the need to scale QCNNs beyond classical simulation limits. It details the novel hierarchical partitioning approach using convolutional and pooling circuits. The core is demonstrating the feasibility of simulating large systems (up to 128 qubits) via a binary reduction tree, culminating in performance comparisons showing maintained or improved accuracy.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

This work presents a study of an implementation of a novel Quantum Convolutional Neural Network (QCNN) for binary classification of images from the Modified National Institute of Standards and Technology (MNIST) dataset. Using a novel architecture inspired by previous QCNN and classical convolutional neural network (CNN) implementations, we use a hierarchical partitioning approach to implement a QCNN circuit that can be approximated and simulated efficiently on a classical machine for a large problem. First, the original image is partitioned such that each process handles a smaller portion of the image, which is encoded into independent states. Then, these partitions merge and combine, resulting in states that contain information from both partitions while halving the number of processes. After repeating this until one process remains, we reduce the dimensionality of the state until a single qubit remains for measurement. Using this approach, we can use multiple processes in parallel to simulate a large QCNN program without the need for exponentially growing hardware requirements as the number of qubits increases. In our work, we use this scheme to train a 128-qubit model, which is impossible to run on any classical supercomputer without the novel architecture. We also explore the impact of this new model architecture on prediction accuracy by training it to perform binary classification on the MNIST dataset with a small number of qubits, and comparing it to a model without partitioning. Our initial findings show that partitioning images into smaller sub-images with this architecture does not degrade the model's performance and sometimes even improves it, likely because it reduces the Barren plateaus issue in the partitioning process.

</details>

### [Benchmarking Large Language Models on Repairing Qiskit Programs using Bugs4Q](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09007v1)

**Authors:** Saumya Brahmbhatt, Mitali Hukkeri, Dongchan Kim, M. V. Panduranga Rao, Lei Zhang  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.09007v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<div class="figure-strip-hint">↔ Scroll figures horizontally</div>
<div class="paper-figures-scroll">
<figure class="paper-figure-card">
<img src="2607.09007_figures/2607.09007_fig1.jpg" alt="Figure 1">
<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 1.</b> Fig. 1. pass@10 by Qiskit version for all four models. Every model peaks at the pre-1.0 release 0.45.0 and drops sharply across the Qiskit 1.0 boundary (dashed line), then continues to erode through the 2.x releases.</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This paper evaluates how well large language models can fix bugs in quantum circuits written using Qiskit. The authors find that performance metrics are misleading because the underlying benchmark itself becomes invalid as the quantum framework updates. They conclude that robust evaluation requires explicitly pinning both the benchmark and the software version.

**Why it may be interesting.** It establishes rigorous standards for evaluating AI assistance in quantum software development, which is crucial for any field relying on complex quantum circuit implementation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study benchmarks Large Language Models (LLMs) on automatically repairing quantum programs written in Qiskit using the Bugs4Q dataset.

**Main result.** LLM performance is highly dependent on the specific version of the underlying quantum framework (Qiskit), and many apparent successes are due to environmental compatibility rather than true repair correctness.

**Method.** The authors evaluate four LLMs by generating multiple repair candidates for 67 known defects across six pinned versions of Qiskit, using pass@k metrics.

**Model / system.** The system under test is quantum computation implemented via Qiskit programs containing real, documented defects sourced from the Bugs4Q benchmark.

**Key observables.** Pass@10 rate, executability status across different Qiskit versions, and the proportion of successful repairs occurring on invalid benchmark entries.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Six pinned Qiskit releases (e.g., 0.25.0 to 2.3.1), and the number of repair candidates tested per defect (up to 10).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption challenged is that benchmark labels remain valid across software updates; correctness depends on the (benchmark, version) pair.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the pass@10 rate peaking at Qiskit 0.45.0 and declining sharply after the Qiskit 1.0 transition, while Table III shows performance restricted to valid pairs.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem of benchmarking quantum code repair, details the version-pinned evaluation protocol using Bugs4Q across multiple LLMs, presents results showing version dependency, and concludes by advocating for mandatory benchmark validation before repair assessment.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In quantum programs, Bugs4Q is a widely used benchmark containing real quantum defects. However, its evaluation assumes that benchmark labels remain valid and that generated fixes execute in the target environment. We evaluate two Bugs4Q versions containing 67 unique real Qiskit defects, adding executable tests where missing, and re-run all entries across six pinned Qiskit releases (0.25.0, 0.45.0, 1.0.0, 1.1.1, 2.0.0, and 2.3.1). We find that quantum benchmarks can suffer from silent label inversion: entries become invalid without errors when reference fixes stop executing or buggy programs no longer reproduce failures. Thus, correctness depends on the (benchmark, version) pair rather than the benchmark alone. We evaluate four LLMs (GPT-4o-mini, GPT-5o-mini, GPT-5.4, and GPT-5.4-mini), generating up to 10 repair candidates per defect and testing them across all versions. GPT-5.4 achieves the highest pass@10 (48.8%), followed by GPT-5.4-mini (47.3%), GPT-5o-mini (30.3%), and GPT-4o-mini (22.6%). All models perform best on Qiskit 0.45.0 and decline after the Qiskit 1.0 transition. Many failures arise from deprecated or incompatible APIs rather than incorrect repairs, and 64\% of successful repairs occur on entries invalid under the target version. We release a re-validated, version-pinned Bugs4Q benchmark and show that benchmark validation must precede repair evaluation.

</details>

**Summary.** This work introduces a constraint-preserving Quantum Approximate Optimization Algorithm (QAOA) framework tailored for personnel rostering. Instead of using penalty terms that allow exploration of invalid states, the authors embed hard constraints directly into the mixer Hamiltonian via guarded and tight-pattern mixers. This guarantees that the quantum evolution remains confined to only feasible schedules, leading to higher quality optimization results.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete, mathematically rigorous technique for enforcing hard constraints in quantum optimization algorithms, which is crucial for applying QAOA to real-world scheduling problems where infeasible states must be strictly avoided.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses how to apply the Quantum Approximate Optimization Algorithm (QAOA) to solve complex, constrained combinatorial optimization problems like personnel rostering.

**Main result.** The authors propose a novel constraint-preserving QAOA framework that embeds hard scheduling constraints directly into the mixer Hamiltonian, guaranteeing feasible evolution by construction.

**Method.** They develop specific mixer constructions—the guarded-XY and tight-pattern extensions—to confine the quantum dynamics exclusively to the subspace of physically valid solutions.

**Model / system.** The model is a binary rostering problem formulated on a transition graph, requiring satisfaction of daily coverage and no-consecutive-duty constraints. The physics involves designing specialized mixer Hamiltonians ($\hat{H}_M$) for QAOA.

**Key observables.** Output distribution quality (concentration on optimal feasible schedules), feasibility probability (aiming for unit probability by construction).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The workload parameters ($W_d$), the size of the roster ($N$ nurses, $D$ days), and the QAOA depth/mixing angles.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption is that constraints can be perfectly encoded into the mixer Hamiltonian to restrict dynamics, distinguishing this from penalty-based methods.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates the binary encoding of a personnel roster for $N=4$ nurses and $D=4$ days.

**Paper structure.** The paper contrasts conventional penalty-based QAOA approaches with their novel constraint-preserving method. It details the construction of guarded and tight-pattern mixers, proves feasibility preservation by construction, and validates the approach via statevector simulations against existing formulations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Quantum Approximate Optimization Algorithm (QAOA) is a promising framework for combinatorial optimization, but constrained problems are commonly handled using energetic penalty terms that require calibration and allow infeasible configurations to remain dynamically accessible. We develop a constraint-preserving QAOA framework for personnel rostering in which hard scheduling constraints are embedded directly into the mixer Hamiltonian. Using a binary rostering model with daily coverage and no-consecutive-duty constraints, we formulate the dynamics from a transition-graph perspective and introduce a guarded-XY mixer that confines the evolution to the fully feasible scheduling manifold. We further distinguish feasibility preservation from feasible-transition design and propose a tight-pattern extension that introduces collective feasible exchanges in saturated workload segments where local guarded exchanges alone are insufficient. Exact statevector simulations demonstrate that, compared with Penalty-X and Coverage-XY formulations under both expectation-value and Conditional Value-at-Risk optimization, the proposed approach eliminates hard-constraint penalty calibration, guarantees feasible evolution by construction, and consistently yields higher-quality output distributions with stronger concentration on optimal feasible schedules. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first constraint-preserving QAOA formulation for personnel rostering, and the transition-graph framework is readily applicable to a broad class of constrained quantum optimization problems.

</details>

### [Is the Eyring Plot Misleading? A Case for Arrhenius Analysis of Activation Parameters](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09341v1)

**Authors:** Titus S. van Erp  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.09341v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<div class="figure-strip-hint">↔ Scroll figures horizontally</div>
<div class="paper-figures-scroll">
<figure class="paper-figure-card">
<img src="2607.09341_figures/2607.09341_fig1.jpg" alt="Figure 1">
<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 1.</b> Figure 1: Two-dimensional potential energy surface. Curvatures along the reaction coordi- nate x in the reactant and product wells are indicated in red. Curvatures along the orthog- onal coordinate y at the reactant, transition, and product states are shown in blue. The hy- pothetical reference curvature kref discussed in the text is indicated by the dashed green line.</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This paper critically examines whether the Eyring or Arrhenius formulation is more fundamentally correct for describing reaction rates. By analyzing theoretical derivations from TST using harmonic and hard-core potentials, the author argues that an Arrhenius analysis yields parameters ($\Delta H^\ddagger, \Delta S^\ddagger$) that are more physically consistent than those derived from the standard Eyring plot due to underlying mathematical inconsistencies.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on chemical kinetics, the rigorous comparison of different theoretical frameworks (Eyring vs. Arrhenius) for extracting fundamental thermodynamic quantities ($\Delta H^\ddagger, \Delta S^\ddagger$) is highly relevant to understanding approximations and limitations in statistical mechanical models used across AMO physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper debates the relative fundamental nature of the Eyring representation versus the Arrhenius representation for calculating chemical reaction rate constants from temperature-dependent measurements.

**Main result.** The author argues that activation enthalpies and entropies derived from an Arrhenius fit are arguably more physically relevant than those obtained directly from the standard Eyring plot analysis due to inherent dimensional mismatches in the free energy of activation ($\Delta G^\ddagger$).

**Method.** The analysis compares rate expressions derived from Transition State Theory (TST), contrasting harmonic approximation results with derivations using reactive flux formalism and proposing a modified potential restraint approach.

**Model / system.** The study models chemical reactions using TST, comparing the standard Eyring equation ($k \propto e^{-eta \Delta G^\ddagger}$) against the Arrhenius form ($k \propto e^{eta E_a}$). It utilizes harmonic and hard-core potential approximations to analyze the temperature dependence of activation parameters.

**Key observables.** Rate constant ($k$), Activation Enthalpy ($\Delta H^\ddagger$), Activation Entropy ($\Delta S^\ddagger$), Gibbs Free Energy of Activation ($\Delta G^\ddagger$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature ($T$), Planck's constant ($h$), Boltzmann constant ($k_B$), and various potential energy surface parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on harmonic approximations for potentials, assumes the validity of TST derivations, and proposes modifications (like using an infinite square-well restraint) to improve parameter consistency.

**Figures summary.** Figure S1 illustrates a particle confined in a water slab between two membranes, representing a hard-core system used in model calculations.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the conflict between Eyring and Arrhenius interpretations; it then details derivations using classical statistical mechanics and reactive flux expressions; finally, it proposes an alternative framework using modified potential restraints to yield consistent Arrhenius parameters.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

A common view in physical chemistry literature is that the Eyring representation, in which a linear fit of $\ln(k/T)$ versus $1/T$ is attempted, is more fundamental than the Arrhenius representation, $\ln(k)$ versus $1/T$. This perception is typically motivated by its derivation from statistical mechanics and quantum mechanics, and by the interpretation of the intercept in terms of the activation entropy $ΔS^\ddagger$, whereas the Arrhenius equation and its prefactor are often regarded as purely phenomenological. However, harmonic approximation models yield exact linearity in Arrhenius plots but not in Eyring plots, although for real experimental data both generally appear equally linear within typical experimental accuracy. Furthermore, the impression that the Eyring formulation is inherently quantum mechanical arises from the presence of the Planck constant in the prefactor, whereas this term results from normalization conventions in the partition function. This also highlights an interpretational issue in $ΔG^\ddagger$, which is based on partition functions of different dimensionality between reactant and transition state. This dimensional mismatch can be reformulated in an alternative representation that improves interpretability and reduces to an Arrhenius-type expression in which the prefactor is directly related to an entropy of activation. In this framework, both activation enthalpy and entropy obtained from an Arrhenius fit are arguably more physically relevant than the corresponding Eyring fit values.

</details>

### [Quantum Logic as the Logic of Contexts](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09032v1)

**Authors:** Haruki Emori, Atsushi Iriki, Andrei Khrennikov, Kazunori Kondo  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.09032v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

**Summary.** This paper redefines quantum logic by treating classical logic as a derived, information-losing image of a richer contextual calculus built on an orthomodular lattice. Using a component-wise algebra over contexts and bit vectors, the authors rigorously demonstrate that contextuality is fundamental, making classical reasoning a specific, restricted limit case.

**Why it may be interesting.** While highly abstract, this work provides a rigorous algebraic framework for understanding how quantum non-classicality (contextuality) structures logical inference beyond what is captured by standard Boolean or even orthomodular logics. It offers deep structural insights into the relationship between context and truth values.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To reverse the conventional understanding of quantum logic, arguing that classical logic is a quotient of a richer contextual quantum logic rather than being its foundation.

**Main result.** The paper proves that classical logic emerges as an information-losing, six-to-one image (a surjective homomorphism) from the full contextual calculus defined by the free orthomodular lattice on two generators.

**Method.** The authors develop a context–bit-vector calculus based on decomposing the 96-element free orthomodular lattice into a direct product of a context factor and a Boolean content factor.

**Model / system.** The model is an abstract algebraic structure: the free orthomodular lattice $F_2$ (with 96 elements), which is mathematically decomposed as $MO_2 	imes 2^4$. The 'contexts' are represented by the non-distributive factor $MO_2$, and the 'content' by the Boolean factor $2^4$.

**Key observables.** Contextuality (dependence on context/order), commutativity of sublogics, and the structure of the projection onto classical logic.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The size of the lattice (96 elements); the number of contexts (six layers derived from $MO_2$); the dimension of the Boolean content (four bits).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Classical logic is treated as a limit or image resulting from 'forgetting' context, and structural analogies to physical processes like measurement are used.

**Figures summary.** The notes reference Table I showing the six-layer stratification of $F_2$ based on contexts, and Appendix A enumerating all 96 elements organized into these layers.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the algebraic model ($F_2 \sim MO_2 	imes 2^4$), defines component-wise operations in the context–bit-vector calculus, classifies the resulting six layers based on commutativity, and culminates by proving the existence of the context-forgetting projection onto classical logic.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum logic is usually presented as a non-classical departure from ordinary reasoning forced on us by quantum mechanics, with classical logic kept as the secure starting point. We argue for the opposite order of explanation in a finite and fully computable setting. The free orthomodular lattice on two generators has ninety-six elements, the direct product of a six-element non-distributive factor and a sixteen-element Boolean factor. Reading the first factor as a register of contexts and the second as Boolean content, we obtain a calculus whose elements are context--bit-vector pairs and whose operations act component by component. With this calculus we establish three results. First, we classify the six layers by commutativity, identifying the central kernel of context-neutral propositions together with a dual central layer in which all complementary contexts are present. Second, we show that orthocomplementation rearranges the layers exactly as the complementation of the small factor rearranges its elements, which makes the duality among the layers rigid rather than accidental. Third, we prove that the operation forgetting the context is a surjective homomorphism of orthocomplemented lattices whose quotient is the classical Boolean algebra, so that classical logic is a six-to-one, information-losing image of the contextual calculus.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates the source of water's anomalous heat capacity by linking it directly to structural fluctuations between low-density and high-density local arrangements. Using molecular dynamics simulations, the authors show that these structural transitions, quantified by an order parameter, provide a microscopic explanation for both the supercooled maximum and the excess ambient $C_P$. The findings establish a quantitative link between liquid structure and macroscopic thermodynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter thermodynamics, the rigorous separation of vibrational vs. configurational contributions and the use of advanced potentials/quantum corrections provide methodological insights relevant to modeling complex quantum many-body systems where structural changes drive macroscopic properties.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to elucidate the structural origin responsible for water's anomalously large isobaric heat capacity ($C_P$) and its characteristic maximum upon supercooling.

**Main result.** The anomalous $C_P$ is primarily attributed to structural fluctuations, quantified by a second-solvation-shell intruder order parameter ($	ext{SSSI}(3)$), which maps onto an effective enthalpy scale of $\approx 4 	ext{ kJ/mol}$ governing the interconversion between low- and high-density-like local structures.

**Method.** The research employs molecular dynamics simulations, comparing classical and path-integral approaches to separate vibrational contributions from structural (configurational) ones. A two-state mapping is used to model the structural transitions.

**Model / system.** The system studied is liquid water ($	ext{H}_2	ext{O}$), simulated using accurate machine-learning interatomic potentials (e.g., NEP3@MB-pol). The analysis focuses on local structural descriptors distinguishing between low-density and high-density environments.

**Key observables.** Isobaric Heat Capacity ($C_P$), second-solvation-shell intruder order parameter ($	ext{SSSI}(3)$), fraction of HDL-like water ($\lambda(T)$), and the derivative $\partial\langle	ext{SSSI}(3)angle / \partial T$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Effective enthalpy scale ($\Delta H_{	ext{L/H}}$) of $\approx 3-4 	ext{ kJ mol}^{-1}$; temperature regimes around supercooling and ambient conditions.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The structural anomaly can be effectively modeled using a two-state approximation describing the interconversion between LDL-like and HDL-like local structures. Quantum effects are treated via path integrals or vibrational density of states corrections.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare calculated $C_P$ (Classical MD vs. PIMD) against experiments, showing that structural derivatives ($\partial\langle	ext{SSSI}(3)angle / \partial T$) track the $C_P$ maximum. Other figures illustrate $\lambda(T)$ and the temperature dependence of structural descriptors.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes $C_P$ using molecular dynamics, first addressing quantum corrections (Classical vs. PIMD). It then introduces a structural order parameter ($	ext{SSSI}(3)$) to decompose $C_P$, showing that the temperature dependence of this structure parameter directly correlates with the anomalous heat capacity maximum.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Water isobaric heat capacity is anomalously large under ambient conditions and exhibits a sharp maximum upon supercooling. Using classical and path-integral molecular dynamics with accurate machine-learning interatomic potentials, we show that nuclear quantum effects primarily act by suppressing high-frequency vibrations, while the anomalous temperature dependence of the isobaric heat capacity originates from structural fluctuations, quantified by the second-solvent-shell intruder order parameter. A simple two-state mapping reveals an effective enthalpy scale of about 4 kJ/mol associated with the interconversion of low- and high-density-like local structures, providing a microscopic link between their population changes and the excess heat capacity from supercooled to ambient conditions.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces QADAPT, a novel Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning framework designed to automate the calibration of large quantum processors. By learning an adaptive, factored representation of the control space, it effectively decouples agent actions despite strong physical cross-talk. This allows for rapid, scalable tuning that generalizes robustly to larger devices without retraining.

**Why it may be interesting.** The core technique—using learned factorizations in MARL to handle complex, coupled control landscapes—is highly relevant for controlling open quantum systems where parameter interactions are non-trivial and scale poorly with device size.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing a scalable method for tuning large quantum devices by overcoming challenges like parameter cross-talk and non-stationarity in multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL).

**Main result.** The proposed framework achieves zero-shot generalization to unseen device sizes and maintains a constant number of convergence steps, providing a scalable route for rapid calibration.

**Method.** It uses an online learned factored representation of the action space within a MARL framework (QADAPT) to decouple agent control and minimize interference during tuning.

**Model / system.** The work applies to electrostatically-defined quantum-dot arrays (semiconductor spin qubits) and superconducting transmon qubits. Tuning involves calibrating gate voltages or controlling qubit parameters like frequency and coupling strengths.

**Key observables.** Charge stability diagrams (CSDs), all-XY output patterns, target charge occupations, and tunnel couplings.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Gate voltages, inter-dot capacitances, bare frequencies ($\omega_{01}$), anharmonicity ($\alpha$), and cross-talk coupling strengths ($\lambda_{ij}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The model assumes that parameter updates can be locally approximated or linearized for the purpose of defining a separable action space; it also notes neglecting full flux noise effects in one context.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate algorithmic flow, Kalman filter performance for cross-capacitance estimation, and convergence plots showing QADAPT's superior performance across increasing qubit/dot counts compared to classical optimizers.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem of large-scale quantum device tuning under cross-talk. It proposes QADAPT, detailing its adaptive action-space factorization using online capacitance estimation (Kalman filter). The method is then applied sequentially to two physical systems: quantum dot arrays and superconducting transmon qubits, demonstrating scalability and convergence.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Cooperative multi-agent reinforcement learning is well suited to problems with large parameter spaces and exploitable local structure, such as the tuning of electrostatically-defined quantum-dot arrays. However, if parameter cross-talk is strong, a non-stationary environment from the perspective of any individual agent can destabilize learning - the same effect that plagues manual tuning of such systems. We propose using a factored representation of the action space, learned online, to decouple agents and minimize their interference. Our framework, QADAPT, uses this factorization to efficiently learn shared policies based on local measurements and rewards. With this modular strategy, we achieve zero-shot generalization to unseen quantum device sizes and maintain an approximately constant number of convergence steps to reach target regimes. This work provides a scalable route toward the rapid calibration of large-scale quantum processors.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper demonstrates how the Casimir interaction mediated by itinerant fermions in mesoscopic rings can be controlled using the Aharonov-Bohm effect. This tunable, long-range interaction provides a novel platform to implement non-Clifford SWAP^alpha quantum gates between distant spin qubits, thereby advancing solid-state quantum computation architectures.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges condensed matter physics (Casimir forces, mesoscopic transport) directly into quantum information science by providing a physical mechanism for long-range qubit coupling that is controllable via external fields.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The goal is to engineer long-range interactions necessary for implementing non-Clifford SWAP^alpha gates between spatially separated spin qubits.

**Main result.** The fermion-mediated Casimir interaction, termed the Aharonov-Bohm–Casimir (ABC) effect, provides a tunable, long-range ($\sim l^{-1}$) mechanism to achieve this gate operation.

**Method.** The authors derive an effective interaction Hamiltonian from the fermionic Green's function formalism and analyze its dependence on the magnetic flux via the Aharonov-Bohm phase.

**Model / system.** The system consists of mesoscopic rings hosting itinerant electrons (fermions) coupled to localized spin-1/2 moments acting as qubits. The physics is governed by the interplay between the Casimir interaction and the AB effect.

**Key observables.** Casimir interaction strength, differential conductance G, and the resulting gate parameter alpha ($\alpha$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Magnetic flux $\Phi$ (or dimensionless phase $	heta$), electron hopping parameters ($J_t, J_r$), and the ring length $l$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on a weak-coupling expansion of the interaction energy formula.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the mesoscopic ring setup. Figure 2 shows the ABC interaction strength dependence on flux $	heta$, comparing theoretical approximations. Figure 3 schematics detail the measurement process using differential conductance to extract coupling parameters for gate implementation.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical platform (mesoscopic rings) and the mechanism (ABC effect), derives the effective Hamiltonian, analyzes its tunability via magnetic flux ($	heta$), and finally demonstrates how this tunable interaction can be used to realize specific quantum gates like $	ext{SWAP}^\alpha$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Casimir effect is typically governed by intrinsic material properties and lacks in situ tunability. We show that, in mesoscopic rings, both the magnitude and sign of the fermion-mediated Casimir interaction can be controlled via the Aharonov-Bohm effect. The resulting interplay between the Aharonov-Bohm phase and the Casimir interaction provides a route to engineer long-range interactions. In particular, this mechanism enables the implementation of non-Clifford SWAP$^α$ gates between spatially separated spin qubits, thereby reducing the overhead for universal quantum computation and quantum error correction in spin-qubit architectures.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proves that chemical reaction-diffusion oscillators can function as a novel platform for generating frequency combs. By analyzing the Brusselator model mathematically and testing it experimentally with the BZ reaction, the authors show that nonlinear kinetics drive the system into a state exhibiting multiple, harmonically related spectral peaks. This bridges concepts from optics/solid-state physics into chemical dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** While not directly quantum, the underlying principles of pattern formation, nonlinear dynamics, and self-organization in non-equilibrium systems are highly relevant to understanding open quantum systems, dissipative quantum computing models, and driven many-body physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates whether chemical reaction-diffusion oscillators can generate frequency combs, a spectral phenomenon previously observed in optics and other physical systems.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate that the Brusselator model, representing a chemical system, naturally produces a clear fundamental frequency followed by an evenly spaced ladder of harmonics above the Hopf bifurcation threshold. This was confirmed both through numerical simulation and experimental measurements using the Belousov-Zhabotinsky reaction.

**Method.** The work combines analytical stability analysis (deriving Hopf bifurcation conditions from rate equations) with time-series simulations and direct optical measurement techniques (FFT analysis of intensity traces).

**Model / system.** The primary model used is the Brusselator kinetics, a well-known reaction-diffusion system. The experimental realization tested this concept using the Belousov-Zhabotinsky (BZ) reaction.

**Key observables.** Frequency comb structure (fundamental frequency and equally spaced harmonics), Hopf bifurcation thresholds, time evolution of intensity patterns, and FFT spectra.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Reactant concentrations ([A], [B]), kinetic parameters ($k_1$ through $k_4$), and the control parameter sweeps used to cross the stability boundaries.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on linear stability theory around a steady state (Hopf bifurcation) and assumes that the trimolecular autocatalytic term is the sole source of nonlinear harmonic content.

**Figures summary.** Figures show target wave patterns, time evolution across two distinct oscillation regimes due to reagent depletion, and corresponding FFT spectra confirming the equally spaced spectral grid structure.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the context by reviewing frequency combs in other fields. It then mathematically analyzes the Hopf bifurcation conditions for the Brusselator model using parameter sweeps. Finally, it validates these findings experimentally using the BZ reaction, comparing simulated and measured harmonic spectra.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Frequency combs, evenly spaced spectral lines locked to one fundamental frequency, are well known in optics and have also been found in phononic, magnonic, ferroelectric, and cosmological systems, but have not yet been studied in oscillating chemical reactions. In this work, we show that reaction-diffusion oscillators can also produce frequency combs. We use the Brusselator model and derive its Hopf bifurcation condition directly from the rate equations. We find that the trimolecular autocatalytic term is the only source of nonlinear harmonic content. Above the Hopf threshold, our simulations of target-wave patterns show a clear fundamental frequency followed by a long, evenly spaced ladder of harmonics, with each harmonic weaker than the one before it. We then vary the reactant concentrations and kinetic parameters one at a time and find that this comb structure holds across a wide range of values. We also test this idea experimentally using the Belousov-Zhabotinsky reaction. Intensity signals recorded at different points in a target pattern show a shared fundamental frequency with several weakening harmonics, matching the simulated pattern closely. Together, these results show that reaction-diffusion chemistry is a new platform for generating frequency combs.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates the mechanism behind signal enhancement in molecule-metal junctions using plasmonics. By systematically tuning the electronic structure of attached molecules, the authors demonstrate that the enhancement is governed by a precise charge-transfer resonance condition dependent on both the metal work function and the excitation light energy. This provides an experimentally accessible framework for engineering molecular plasmonic devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly bridges concepts from quantum optics and open quantum systems by quantifying how external electromagnetic fields (plasmons) couple to molecular electronic states via resonant charge transfer pathways. The identification of specific resonance conditions provides a quantitative tool for designing molecular plasmonic devices.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study addresses the poorly understood energetic conditions governing charge-transfer resonance within molecule-metal nanocavities, which is critical for plasmon-enhanced spectroscopy.

**Main result.** The research establishes that charge-transfer enhancement in SERS and vSFG is governed by a well-defined interfacial resonance condition dependent on both the substrate work function and the excitation energy.

**Method.** The authors combine experimental techniques (SERS, vSFG) with theoretical calculations (DFT) to probe molecular electronic structure changes across systematically varied molecular libraries.

**Model / system.** The physical system involves plasmonic nanoparticle-on-mirror junctions incorporating engineered biphenylthiol derivatives monolayers. The investigation focuses on tuning the Lowest Unoccupied Molecular Orbital (LUMO) energy of these molecules relative to the metal substrate's electronic levels.

**Key observables.** Raman/SERS intensity variations, vibrational fingerprints, and the correlation between enhancement magnitude and LUMO alignment.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Substrate work function, excitation energy ($	ext{e.g., } 632 	ext{ nm vs. } 785 	ext{ nm}$), and the molecular LUMO energy level.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The study assumes that by varying substituents while keeping the anchoring geometry constant, electronic structure variations can be isolated from structural heterogeneity effects.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate schematic charge transfer processes in Au NPoM junctions and show SERS spectra correlating intensity changes with LUMO energy under different excitation wavelengths.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first identifying the core problem, then detailing the experimental setup using molecular libraries on plasmonic substrates. It presents spectroscopic evidence showing resonance dependence on wavelength (632 nm vs 785 nm), confirming a charge-transfer mechanism, and concludes by establishing an engineering framework for these junctions.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Interfacial charge-transfer processes play a critical role in plasmon-enhanced spectroscopy, yet the energetic conditions governing charge-transfer resonance within molecule-metal nanocavities remain poorly understood. Here, plasmonic nanoparticle-on-mirror junctions incorporating systematically engineered biphenylthiol derivatives monolayers were used to investigate how frontier orbital alignment influences chemical enhancement mechanisms. A molecular library spanning a broad range of electronically tuned acceptor states was examined using surface-enhanced Raman scattering (SERS), vibrational sum frequency generation (vSFG) spectroscopy, and density functional theory calculations. By combining different excitation wavelengths with controlled variation of substrate composition and molecular electronic structure, the energetic relationship between plasmon-enhanced charge transfer excitation and molecular orbital alignment was quantitatively evaluated. The results reveal that charge-transfer enhancement of Raman scattering is governed by a well-defined interfacial resonance condition dependent on substrate work function and excitation energy. We further probe a subset of molecular-metal systems by nanocavity-enhanced vSFG and identify the same resonance conditions as in SERS, consistent with expectations. These findings establish an experimentally accessible framework for probing and engineering charge-transfer processes in plasmonic molecular junctions and provide mechanistic insight relevant to molecular plasmonics, charge carrier photophysics, and nanoscale interfacial spectroscopy.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates using the inherent nonlinearity in Microwave Kinetic Inductance Detectors (MKIDs) as a controllable resource. By tuning the readout power near resonance bifurcation points, the authors achieve significant enhancements in detector responsivity. This work is crucial for improving cryogenic sensor sensitivity by mastering nonlinear operational regimes.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides an excellent example of how nonlinear classical physics (bifurcation theory) can be applied to enhance the sensitivity of quantum-limited detectors, drawing parallels between driven resonator dynamics and open quantum system response functions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research aims to utilize the inherent nonlinearity of Microwave Kinetic Inductance Detectors (MKIDs) by controlling the readout current to achieve tunable responsivity and bandwidth enhancement.

**Main result.** By operating near the resonator's bifurcation point, a factor of 10 enhancement in optical response was demonstrated, although this comes at the cost of reduced bandwidth.

**Method.** The authors developed a theoretical framework based on analyzing the nonlinear steady-state response of the driven resonator, complemented by experimental measurements of noise spectra under varying bias conditions.

**Model / system.** The system is an MKID array composed of superconducting resonators whose kinetic inductance depends nonlinearly on the circulating microwave current. The physics is modeled using a coupled optical cavity approach describing the steady-state photon number balance.

**Key observables.** Responsivity (both optical and microwave), bandwidth, resonant frequency shift ($\delta x$), and noise spectrum characteristics (white noise level, roll-off frequency).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Readout power ($P_g$), detuning ($x_0$), quality factors ($Q_r, Q_c$), and the nonlinear current scale ($I^*$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis simplifies by focusing on reactive inductance changes ($\delta L_k \propto I^2$) while ignoring pair-breaking energy effects. The system is modeled as a one-port optical cavity coupled to a three-port network.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the experimental setup, power sweep responses showing bistability/hysteresis near bifurcation points, and noise spectra confirming enhanced white noise levels and reduced roll-off frequency with increased nonlinear bias.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing MKIDs' nonlinearity as a resource, developing the theoretical model for controlled operation via $(x_0, P_g)$, demonstrating responsivity enhancement through bifurcation analysis, and finally validating these effects by analyzing noise spectra changes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Microwave kinetic inductance detectors (MKIDs) are generally read out with microwave readout tones of high enough amplitude to adequately suppress the noise contribution of the first-stage amplifier. At high readout power, the detector's resonant frequency is altered as a result of the dependence of the kinetic inductance on the internally circulating microwave current. With the tone placed below the resonant frequency, the nonlinear frequency shift results in a positive feedback effect that can significantly enhance the responsivity of the detector, to both optical and microwave power. We report a factor of 10 enhancement in optical response by tuning the readout power and frequency to close to the resonator's bifurcation point. A corresponding decrease in the bandwidth of the resonator is observed under these conditions. We show that the strength of the feedback effect can be easily selected by adjusting the excitation, and provide a map of possible operational states to do so. Operation of MKIDs in this mode could be used to improve sensitivity when non-intrinsic noise sources are significant.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work develops a microscopic formalism to analyze the anomalous Hall effect and general optical response in magnetic conductors at finite frequencies. By treating electronic degrees of freedom via second-quantized field theory, the authors decompose the conductivity tensor into three physically distinct parts: polarization (Kubo), Drude, and anomalous Hall contributions. This provides a unified dynamic picture for charge transport beyond static DC measurements.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter transport, the decomposition of conductivity into fundamental responses (polarization vs. free current) provides a rigorous framework for understanding linear response in magnetically ordered systems, which has parallels to open quantum system descriptions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper revisits the anomalous Hall effect (AHE) in magnetic conductors, generalizing its study to finite frequencies using a microscopic formalism.

**Main result.** The long-wavelength conductivity tensor is shown to be a sum of three distinct contributions: a Kubo term from polarization response, and two terms (Drude and anomalous Hall) from the free current response.

**Method.** The analysis employs second-quantized field theory, decomposing charge/current into microscopic polarization, magnetization, and free components derived via lattice gauge theory concepts.

**Model / system.** The system is magnetic conductors with long-range magnetic order, modeled using an electronic Hamiltonian incorporating a static, cell-periodic magnetic field that breaks time-reversal symmetry. Calculations are performed for the bcc phase of iron.

**Key observables.** Long-wavelength conductivity tensor $\sigma(\omega)$, polarization ($\mathbf{P}$), magnetization ($\mathbf{M}$), and free charge/current densities ($ho_F, \mathbf{j}_F$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Finite frequency ($\omega$), magnetic field strength (encoded in the Hamiltonian), and characteristic length scales ($\Delta$ for spatial averaging).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis uses a frozen-ion approximation ($\Gamma$ fixed) and relies on long-wavelength approximations.

**Figures summary.** Not specified by notes, but calculations are presented for iron's bcc phase showing frequency dependence of conductivity contributions.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds the formalism by defining microscopic quantities (polarization, magnetization) and then derives the macroscopic response ($\sigma(\omega)$) by combining three distinct theoretical terms: Kubo, Drude, and anomalous Hall components.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We revisit the anomalous Hall effect in magnetic conductors, and its generalization to finite frequencies, using a formalism based on microscopic notions of polarization, magnetization, and free charges and currents. The electronic degrees of freedom are treated within second-quantized field theory, where the Hamiltonian features a static and cell-periodic magnetic field that encodes the magnetic order in the crystal and breaks time-reversal symmetry. We study the dynamics of bound and free charge carriers at the microscopic level as they respond to a spatially uniform electric field at finite frequency. The conductivity tensor describing the long-wavelength response is a sum of three terms, including a Kubo term associated with the polarization response, along with the metallic Drude term and the anomalous Hall conductivity that are associated with the longitudinal and transverse parts of the free current response, respectively. We also present numerical calculations of these contributions for the ferromagnetic body-centered cubic phase of iron.

</details>

### [Neutron contribution to the force on a proton vortex in superconducting neutron-star matter](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09468v1)

**Authors:** Oleg A. Goglichidze, Mikhail E. Gusakov  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.09468v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **3/5** · `non-equilibrium dynamics` **3/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **1/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **1/5** · `non-equilibrium universality` **1/5**

</figure>

**Summary.** This paper calculates the force acting on a proton vortex embedded in superconducting neutron star material. It specifically derives and quantifies a previously uncounted longitudinal force component arising from normal neutrons interacting via Fermi-liquid coupling with superconducting protons. This work advances our understanding of vortex dynamics in compact objects by incorporating these subtle quantum mechanical interactions.

**Why it may be interesting.** The application of Fermi-liquid theory to determine transport forces in extreme astrophysical environments like neutron stars provides a rich example of non-equilibrium many-body physics relevant to dense matter dynamics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the additional force exerted on a proton vortex within superconducting neutron-star matter due to interactions with normal neutrons.

**Main result.** Normal neutrons contribute a new longitudinal force component to the vortex force, which is proportional to the relative neutron-vortex velocity and vanishes without Fermi-liquid coupling.

**Method.** The analysis employs a kinetic approach derived from Landau Fermi-liquid theory applied to the neutron-proton subsystem interactions.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is dense matter inside a neutron star, comprising superconducting protons, normal neutrons, and electrons. The physics involves type-II superconductivity characterized by magnetic vortices.

**Key observables.** Total force on the vortex ($F_{m	o v}$), which is expected to be longitudinal and velocity-dependent.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature relative to critical temperatures ($T_{cn} < T < T_{cp}$); characteristic length scales like coherence length ($\xi$) and London penetration depth ($\lambda$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Protons are superconducting while neutrons are normal; the vortex core is treated as infinitely thin, and quasiparticle collisions are neglected.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the force calculation by analyzing momentum conservation laws ($\partial P_i / \partial t + \partial \Pi_{ik} / \partial r_k = ...$) and decomposing the total force into contributions from different particle species, culminating in the derivation of the neutron contribution $\mathbf{F}^{(np)}_i$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the forces acting on a proton vortex in superconducting neutron-star matter composed of neutrons, protons, and electrons, accounting for Fermi-liquid interactions in the neutron-proton subsystem. While the force arising from electron scattering by the vortex magnetic field is well known, we demonstrate that normal neutrons also exert a force on proton vortices due to Fermi-liquid coupling with superconducting protons. Using a kinetic approach based on Landau Fermi-liquid theory, we show that neutron quasiparticles scatter off a proton vortex through the spatially varying condensate momentum, giving rise to a longitudinal force proportional to the relative neutron-vortex velocity. In contrast to the electron contribution, this force has no transverse component and vanishes in the absence of neutron-proton Fermi-liquid interaction. Within a simple model, we analyze the main properties of this force and provide physically motivated estimates of its magnitude.

</details>
