**Summary.** This work theoretically investigates $	ext{MSe}_2/	ext{WTe}_2$ van der Waals heterobilayers to engineer next-generation spintronic materials. By substituting W with various 3d metals, the researchers demonstrate that interface formation induces favorable type-II band alignment and dramatically enhances perpendicular magnetic anisotropy. The findings establish these heterostructures as promising candidates for low-dimensional electronic and magnetic devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter magnetism and electronics, the detailed theoretical modeling of interfacial coupling mechanisms—which dictate emergent quantum phenomena like spin-selectivity and enhanced anisotropy—is highly relevant to understanding engineered quantum interfaces.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how interface engineering in van der Waals heterobilayers can enhance electronic properties, specifically achieving spin-resolved type-II band alignment and robust perpendicular magnetic anisotropy for spintronic applications.

**Main result.** The $	ext{MSe}_2/	ext{WTe}_2$ heterobilayers exhibit significant improvements upon interface formation, with $	ext{FeSe}_2/	ext{WTe}_2$ showing simultaneous half-metallicity, type-II alignment, and enhanced magnetic anisotropy.

**Method.** The research employs first-principles Density Functional Theory (DFT) calculations, supplemented by methods to calculate Magnetic Anisotropy Energy (MAE) and model finite-temperature magnetism using Monte Carlo simulations.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the $	ext{MSe}_2/	ext{WTe}_2$ van der Waals heterobilayer, where M is a 3d transition metal ($	ext{V}, 	ext{Cr}, 	ext{Mn}, 	ext{Fe}, 	ext{Co}$). The analysis focuses on structural stability, electronic band structure, and magnetic coupling across the heterointerface.

**Key observables.** Type-II band gap (e.g., 0.70 eV), half-metallicity, Perpendicular Magnetic Anisotropy Energy (MAE), Curie Temperature ($T_C$), and built-in electric fields.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The study compares various transition metals (V to Co) and stacking configurations ($	ext{AA}'$ preferred). Key energy scales include MAE values (e.g., $20.8 	ext{ meV}$ for $	ext{MnSe}_2$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Calculations rely on DFT approximations (GGA+U), and magnetic behavior at finite temperatures is modeled using classical Heisenberg Hamiltonians.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate stacking sequences, band structures showing type-II alignment, charge density redistribution profiles indicating built-in fields, and angular dependence of MAE.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the structural stability, electronic properties (band alignment/charge transfer), and magnetic anisotropy enhancement across different $	ext{M}$ substitutions in the heterobilayer system.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Two-dimensional van der Waals heterobilayers provide an attractive platform for the development of next-generation spintronic devices. Here, first-principles calculations are performed to investigate the structural, electronic and magnetic properties of MSe2/WTe2 (M = V, Cr, Mn, Fe, and Co) van der Waals heterobilayers. The pristine WSe2/WTe2 heterobilayer in AA'-configuration is found to be energetically favorable and exhibits type-II band alignment with a band gap of 0.70 eV, and this provides an ideal platform for controlling carrier transport. Substituting W with 3d transition metal atoms, induces long-range magnetic ordering and reconstructs the spin-resolved electronic band structure. The formation of the heterointerface generates pronounced charge redistribution and an intrinsic built-in electric field, leading to interface-induced electronic reconstruction. MnSe2/WTe2 heterobilayer exhibits half-metallicity, whereas FeSe2/WTe2 heterobilayer simultaneously exhibits half-metallicity and spin-resolved type-II band alignment. Interfacial electronic reconstruction further produces a substantial perpendicular magnetic anisotropy, driving MnSe2 from an in-plane easy axis with MAE value of 1.10 meV in the isolated monolayer to a robust out-of-plane easy axis with MAE value of 20.8 meV in the heterobilayer. Among all the structures, CoSe2/WTe2 heterobilayer exhibits maximum Curie temperature (273.87 K). The combined results establish that interface engineering makes MSe2/WTe2 heterobilayers as a promising candidates for next-generation low-dimensional spintronic applications.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper presents plasmon-enhanced nonlinear vibrational spectroscopy as a novel technique to study molecules adsorbed inside metallic nanogaps. By measuring the ratio of sum-frequency generation to difference-frequency generation signals, researchers can unambiguously determine the relative orientation of molecules with few-molecule sensitivity. This method offers a powerful tool for molecular electronics and surface science.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful spectroscopic tool for probing interfacial chemistry at the nanoscale, which is highly relevant to understanding quantum phenomena in molecular junctions and energy transfer processes at interfaces.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is monitoring side-specific molecular interactions and determining relative molecular orientation within metallic nanogaps due to their buried nature.

**Main result.** The authors successfully developed a method that determines molecular orientation and preferential binding sites with few-molecule sensitivity, revealing that the non-resonant electronic response is governed by molecule-surface interaction.

**Method.** They introduce plasmon-enhanced nonlinear vibrational spectroscopy, utilizing simultaneous measurement of vibrational sum-frequency generation (vSFG) and difference-frequency generation (vDFG).

**Model / system.** The experiment uses metallic nanogaps (e.g., gold-molecule-gold junctions) within a dual-resonant nanocavity setup, enabling the study of molecular adsorption on double interfaces.

**Key observables.** The ratio $R(\omega) = |\chi^{(2)}_{	ext{eff}}(-\omega)|^2 / |\chi^{(2)}_{	ext{eff}}(+\omega)|$ is the key diagnostic observable used to infer molecular orientation ($\phi_j$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Few-molecule sensitivity, continuous-wave excitation, and the relative phase shift between adsorbed molecules (e.g., parallel vs. anti-parallel).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on separating the total nonlinear response into resonant ($\chi^{(2)}_{	ext{Res}}$) and non-resonant ($\chi^{(2)}_{	ext{NR}}$) contributions to extract orientation information.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the experimental setup, model the dependence of the $R(\omega)$ ratio on molecular orientation (e.g., $\phi_j=0$ vs. $\phi_j=\pi$), and show monitoring of molecular infiltration dynamics.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the technique by detailing the physical problem, presents the theoretical framework involving nonlinear susceptibility components, demonstrates its application to determine orientation using $R(\omega)$, and concludes with examples of monitoring diffusion/binding.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Molecule-surface interactions are central to many research and technological areas, spanning from heterogeneous catalysis and polymer science to electrochemistry. Of particular relevance are metallic nanogaps used in molecular electronics and near-field spectroscopy. Due to the buried nature of these double interfaces, few methods exist to monitor side-specific interactions and relative molecular orientation inside the gap. In this work, we introduce plasmon-enhanced nonlinear vibrational spectroscopy as an efficient tool to investigate surface molecular adsorption within metallic nanojunctions. By exploiting simultaneous vibrational sum- and difference-frequency generation in dual-resonant nanocavities, we resolve molecular orientation and preferential binding to one of the two gold surfaces, with few-molecule sensitivity. We also discover that the non-resonant (electronic) second-order nonlinear response is not an intrinsic property of the metal surface, but is instead governed by the molecule-surface interaction. Our findings provide a powerful analytical tool, easily implementable as an add-on to Raman spectroscopy, thanks to commercially available mid-infrared quantum cascade lasers.

</details>

**Summary.** This work proposes using magnetic quantum oscillations to detect a secondary topological invariant—the $\mathbb{Z}_2$ monopole charge ($w_2$)—in nodal-line semimetals. The key diagnostic is observing a specific $\pi$ phase shift in the oscillation signal when the field is applied parallel to the ring axis. Graphdiyne is identified as the most promising material candidate for experimental verification.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter topology, the reliance on precise semiclassical quantization rules and topological invariants makes it relevant for understanding boundary conditions in quantum systems, which has parallels with open quantum system dynamics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to find a measurable bulk transport signature that can distinguish nodal-line semimetals possessing a $\mathbb{Z}_2$ monopole charge ($w_2=1$) from those without ($w_2=0$).

**Main result.** Magnetic quantum oscillations, specifically the phase shift in the oscillatory conductivity when the magnetic field is parallel to the ring axis, can directly diagnose the presence of the $\mathbb{Z}_2$ monopole charge by predicting a topological phase shift of $\pi$.

**Method.** The analysis uses semiclassical theory applied to nodal-line semimetals, calculating quantum oscillations via the Lifshitz–Kosevich formula and extracting topological information from the phase offset ($\gamma$) in the oscillatory conductivity.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on three-dimensional nodal-line semimetals containing 'nodal rings' that can host both a Berry phase ($w_1\pi$) and a $\mathbb{Z}_2$ monopole charge ($w_2$). ABC-stacked graphdiyne is presented as the most promising candidate material.

**Key observables.** Magnetic quantum oscillation phase offset ($\gamma$), which is sensitive to the topological invariant $w_2$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The magnetic field direction relative to the ring axis, and the ratio of SOC scales to characteristic energy scales (e.g., $\Delta\text{SOC}/E_F$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes closed nodal rings with toroidal Fermi surfaces; for material screening, it requires weak Spin-Orbit Coupling (SOC) relative to other energy scales.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the cyclotron orbit geometry showing how $\mathbf{B} \parallel \hat{z}$ leads to a $\pi$ phase shift for $w_2=1$, while $\mathbf{B} \parallel \hat{x}$ shows standard Berry phase behavior. Other figures show FFTs of conductivity and Landau fans.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theoretical framework by deriving continuum Landau spectra, establishing the diagnostic role of the phase shift ($\Phi_{\phi}$) for $\mathbf{B} \parallel \hat{z}$, and then applies this test to screen various material platforms (graphdiyne, TMDs, Si/Ge) based on their predicted SOC scales.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Topological semimetals host band nodes characterized by quantized invariants that can appear in bulk responses, yet some invariants remain hidden from standard probes. In particular, band nodes can carry secondary topological charges whose transport signatures are still largely unexplored. Here we study three-dimensional nodal-line semimetals in which nodal rings carry both the Berry phase $w_1π$ and a $\mathbb{Z}_2$ monopole charge $w_2$. We show that magnetic quantum oscillations, usually treated as a probe of $w_1$, can directly diagnose $w_2$, with the relevant signal selected by the magnetic-field direction. For a field along the ring axis, the inner and outer extremal orbits of the toroidal Fermi surface both encircle the $w_2$-enforced thread and exhibit a topological phase shift $νw_2π$ in the $ν$th harmonic, which is accessible through standard phase-resolved quantum-oscillation analysis. By contrast, for a field applied perpendicular to the ring axis, the relevant extremal orbit exhibits the usual $π$ phase shift associated with the Berry phase $w_1π$, independent of $w_2$. For weak doping, three-dimensional ABC-stacked graphdiyne is predicted to exhibit the proposed oscillations in a field range accessible with present-day high-field facilities.

</details>

**Summary.** This work experimentally and theoretically investigates nonlinear electron transport phenomena, including the Nonlinear Hall Effect, in 2D tellurene driven by THz radiation. The researchers demonstrate that these currents are quadratic in the electric field and can be mapped onto photogalvanic effects arising from intrinsic mechanisms like Berry curvature dipole. The results highlight strong temperature and frequency dependencies, confirming the material's unique electronic response.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state electronics, the study's detailed analysis of symmetry breaking (chirality) and Berry curvature effects in 2D materials under high-frequency fields provides insights relevant to topological physics and advanced quantum transport modeling.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates second-order nonlinear electron transport phenomena, such as the Nonlinear Hall Effect (NLHE), induced by terahertz (THz) radiation in two-dimensional tellurene.

**Main result.** The THz-driven currents are shown to be equivalent to contributions from the Linear Photogalvanic Effect and originate from microscopic mechanisms like skew scattering, side jump, and Berry curvature dipole.

**Method.** A combination of experimental measurements using THz lasers on Hall bar samples and theoretical modeling based on phenomenological and microscopic theories was employed.

**Model / system.** The physical system is two-dimensional tellurene flakes, an elemental semiconductor allotrope. The transport response is modeled by considering the current density as a quadratic function of the incident electric field ($\mathbf{j} \propto E^2$).

**Key observables.** Nonlinear Hall (NLHE), Nonlinear Longitudinal (NLL), and Nonlinear Diagonal (NLD) currents; photocurrent dependence on THz polarization angle ($\alpha$), gate voltage ($U_G$), temperature ($T$), and radiation frequency ($\omega$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature range (300 K down to 4.2 K); THz frequencies (0.6 to 2.54 THz); Gate voltage control ($U_G$) used to tune carrier type/density.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The symmetry of the studied material is reduced to $C_1$, and the current scaling is assumed to be quadratic in the electric field.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate optical micrographs, two-point resistance vs. gate voltage curves used to locate the Charge Neutrality Point (CNP), and plots showing photocurrent dependence on polarization angle ($\alpha$) and radiation intensity ($I$).

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical framework for second-order transport, details the experimental setup using THz irradiation, presents measurements of current dependence on orientation and gate voltage, and finally correlates these findings with established microscopic theories.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study terahertz field-driven second-order nonlinear electron transport phenomena, including the nonlinear Hall effect (NLHE), in two-dimensional tellurene flakes. The dc current excited by linearly polarized terahertz (THz) radiation in Hall bar samples is investigated in directions both along and perpendicular to the $c$-axis of tellurene. As expected for second-order transport phenomena, the current scales as the square of the in-plane electric field of the radiation $\bf E$, and depends on its orientation. The current results from a combination of three contributions, including the NLHE, the Nonlinear Longitudinal (NLL) and Nonlinear Diagonal (NLD) currents. We established the equivalence between NLH, NLL, and NLD transport currents and Linear photogalvanic effect (LPGE) contributions induced by the absorption of linearly polarized and unpolarized THz radiation. All contributions can be controlled by a gate voltage and have opposite signs for electron and hole conductivity. The magnitude of the current increases drastically when the samples are cooled from room temperature to 4.2 K. It also increases with decreasing radiation frequency. These results are well described by the developed phenomenological and microscopic theories. We show that the THz radiation-induced electric current originates from microscopic mechanisms such as skew scattering, side jump, and the Berry curvature dipole.

</details>

### [A Cloud-Accessible Open-Source Framework for the Electromagnetic Modelling of Applied Superconductors](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.09572v1)

**Authors:** Yusen Guo, Alberto Paganini, Harold S. Ruiz  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** numerical methods · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.09572v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5** · `analog quantum simulation` **1/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **1/5** · `spintronics-quantum-optics interface` **1/5**

**Summary.** This work introduces H-cloud, an open-source, cloud-accessible finite-element framework for simulating electromagnetics in applied superconductors. It solves the nonlinear time-dependent PDE using curl-conforming Nédélec elements and Python scripting (Firedrake). The primary achievement is demonstrating that this rigorous methodology yields accurate results while maintaining exceptional portability across diverse computing environments.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical electromagnetism, the paper represents a state-of-the-art example of building highly portable, transparent, and extensible computational infrastructure for complex physical models, which is valuable methodology for quantum simulations.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing a rigorous, reproducible, and portable computational framework for solving the time-dependent electromagnetic problem in applied superconductors.

**Main result.** The open-source workflow successfully reproduces commercial reference magnetisation loops to within 1% accuracy across matched mesh studies, demonstrating high portability between local and cloud computing environments.

**Method.** It employs a finite-element method using Nédélec elements on the H(curl) space, solving the nonlinear time-dependent PDE implicitly via a Python framework (Firedrake/UFL).

**Model / system.** The model concerns Type-II superconductors exhibiting hysteretic magnetization under applied magnetic fields. The governing physics is described by Maxwell's equations coupled with a nonlinear E-J power law constitutive relation.

**Key observables.** Magnetization per unit length (M) and the resulting magnetization loop shape as a function of applied field amplitude.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Critical current density ($J_c$), critical electric field ($E_c$), and the nonlinear exponent ($n$) defining the E-J relation.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The Magneto-Quasi-Steady (MQS) approximation is used, neglecting displacement currents. The use of curl-conforming elements ensures $
abla \cdot \mathbf{B} = 0$ is maintained naturally.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 shows calculated magnetization loops for a cylindrical superconductor benchmark across varying applied field amplitudes using the H-cloud formalism.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the H-cloud formalism, details the weak form derived from Maxwell's equations and nonlinear constitutive laws, presents the implementation using Nédélec elements in Firedrake/UFL, validates it against canonical benchmarks (Bean model, COMSOL), and discusses extensions to adjoint differentiation for optimization.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present the H-cloud formalism, a cloud-accessible and open-source finite-element framework for electromagnetic modelling of applied superconductors. The proposed method expresses the nonlinear electromagnetic response of type-II superconductors in a curl-conforming discretisation based on Nédélec finite elements, where the tangential applied-field boundary condition, nonlinear E-J power law, and fully implicit time-discrete residual are stated explicitly at the variational level, all within a scripted Python finite-element workflow. The weak form is used as the basis for forward simulation and for extension to adjoint differentiation and PDE-constrained optimisation, while keeping the governing assumptions, boundary conditions, and solver structure fully visible to the user. The implementation is realised in Firedrake with UFL and PETSc-backed nonlinear solvers, allowing the identical script to run on local machines and in browser-accessible environments such as Google Colab without reformulating the problem. The method is verified on the canonical magnetisation benchmark of a cylindrical superconductor under Bean-like penetration conditions and then benchmarked against an independently constructed COMSOL model for a practical high temperature superconducting Bi2212 wire. Across matched mesh studies, the open-source workflow reproduces the commercial-reference magnetisation loops to within approximately \(1\%\) , with relative peak errors below 1.5%, while cloud execution preserves the same numerical solution at rather modest additional runtime considering the use of (freely available) reduced hardware resources. The proposed framework provides a rigorous, reproducible, and portable route for superconducting simulation, benchmarking, and future optimisation-led modelling of applied and functional superconductors, shareable and executable into open cloud environments.

</details>

**Summary.** This work reports on the discovery of anisotropic superconductivity in strained $	ext{RuO}_2$ thin films grown epitaxially. By measuring transport properties along different crystallographic directions and applying magnetic fields, the authors demonstrate that strain significantly enhances the critical field beyond theoretical limits. The findings highlight the crucial role of spin-orbit coupling and epitaxial strain in engineering high-performance superconducting materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on unconventional superconductivity, the strong dependence on crystal orientation and strain provides a model for engineering superconducting properties in low-dimensional systems, which is relevant to quantum materials research.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to understand the nature of superconductivity in $	ext{RuO}_2$ and how its properties are governed by epitaxial strain and crystallographic direction.

**Main result.** Superconductivity is strongly anisotropic, exhibiting enhanced critical fields ($H_{c2,\parallel}$) that violate the Pauli paramagnetic limit, which is attributed to strong spin-orbit scattering effects in strained films.

**Method.** The research combines experimental transport measurements (measuring sheet resistance vs. magnetic field along various directions) with theoretical modeling (DFT calculations and weak coupling theory).

**Model / system.** The physical system is epitaxial $	ext{RuO}_2$ thin films grown on different single crystal substrates ($	ext{TiO}_2(100)$ and $	ext{TiO}_2(110)$), allowing for controlled strain engineering.

**Key observables.** Superconducting transition temperature ($T_c$), critical magnetic fields ($H_{c2,\parallel}$ vs $H_{c2,\perp}$), critical current asymmetry, and the ratio of measured field to the Pauli limit ($H_{c2}/H_P$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Epitaxial strain, spin-orbit scattering strength, dimensionality (suggesting 2D superconductivity), and temperature/magnetic field ranges.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis initially relies on weak coupling assumptions, but the strong enhancement suggests mechanisms beyond simple BCS theory are at play.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the dependence of superconducting transitions and critical fields on transport direction and applied in-plane magnetic field. XRD data confirms anisotropic strain relaxation defects.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically investigates how substrate choice dictates superconductivity, measures anisotropy via directional transport measurements, quantifies the violation of fundamental limits (Pauli), and theoretically models the enhancement mechanism using DFT and Ginzburg-Landau approaches.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The superconductivity in RuO2 emerges under strain. RuO2 is also an altermagnet candidate. The nature of superconductivity and its relation with neighboring orders, however, are not understood. To address this problem, we grew epitaxial RuO2 films on TiO2(100) and TiO2(110) single crystal substrates and studied the electronic transport and emergent superconductivity along various crystallographic directions. We show that the superconducting transition strongly depends on the growth orientation and the crystallographic direction of the transport in the RuO2 films. We also observe a strong violation of Pauli paramagnetic limit with in-plane applied magnetic field which we attribute to strong spin-orbit scattering. These results offer opportunities for epitaxially engineered superconductors.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper proposes using quasiparticle steering at Josephson junctions as a novel probe for the Cooper pair momentum. By analyzing how Andreev bound states exit into a normal metal, the authors calculate that the emitted particles exhibit a measurable angle proportional to $(\Delta/\mu)^{1/2}$. This provides a robust, phase-controlled signature detectable via electron optics techniques.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, measurable signature—a directional deflection—for a fundamental superconducting property (Cooper pair momentum), offering an alternative to energy-shift measurements relevant for quantum transport studies in mesoscopic devices.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The work aims to develop a method to kinematically measure the Cooper pair momentum ($\hbar q$) in superconductors by analyzing the direction of quasiparticles ejected from Josephson junctions.

**Main result.** It is shown that Andreev bound states eject into an adjacent normal region at a finite, phase-controlled angle $\Theta \sim (\Delta/\mu)^{1/2}$, which is parametrically larger than the conventional Cooper pair momentum scale ($\Delta/\mu$).

**Method.** The analysis involves solving continuum scattering problems by matching solutions derived from Andreev bound states (ABS) in the superconducting junction to propagating electron modes in the normal region.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is a planar Josephson junction connecting a superconductor (SNS side) to a normal metal lead. The physics is described using linearized Bogoliubov-de Gennes Hamiltonians and perturbative expansions.

**Key observables.** The primary observable is the angle ($\Theta$) of quasiparticle ejection into the normal region, quantified by the ratio of transverse to longitudinal currents ($j_{e,y}/j_{e,x}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The key parameters are the superconducting gap ($\Delta$), chemical potential ($\mu$), and the superconducting phase difference ($\phi$). The critical scale is $\Delta/\mu$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes ballistic transport, a clean planar junction, and utilizes perturbative expansions in the small ratio $\Delta/\mu$ (or $\Delta \ll \mu$).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate electron current density ejected at subgap energies showing finite angle ejection for specific phases ($\phi=\pi/2$), and show tight-binding Andreev bands mapping out the ejection angle $	heta = \arctan(k_y/k_x)$.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the theoretical framework using linearized BdG Hamiltonians, deriving the ABS solutions, solving the continuum scattering problem by matching boundary conditions at the junction interface, and finally calculating the mean transverse momentum of the transmitted field.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Cooper pair momentum in a superconductor is associated with a phase gradient of the superconducting order parameter. In general, this momentum is small compared to the Fermi momentum, which makes it challenging to measure. Josephson junctions, however, enable the creation of large phase gradients and transfer of the Cooper pair momentum to quasiparticles via Andreev reflection. In this work we demonstrate that Andreev bound states propagating along ballistic planar Josephson junctions eject into an adjacent normal region at a phase-controlled angle that scales as $Θ\sim \sqrt{Δ/ μ}$, where $Δ$ is the superconducting gap and $μ$ is the chemical potential. This angle parametrically exceeds the conventional Cooper pair momentum scale $Δ/ μ$, and thus this phenomenon is sizeable even within the Andreev approximation regime $Δ/ μ\ll 1$. Our results establish phase-controlled quasiparticle ejection as a kinematic probe of condensate momentum transfer: unlike existing probes that detect the Doppler energy shift, the signal appears as a momentum-space deflection of emitted quasiparticles.

</details>

**Summary.** This work experimentally investigates whether Aluminum (Al) contributes significant torque via its bulk or interface when placed between magnetic layers like Co and FePt. By measuring harmonic Hall voltages, the authors report that these orbital contributions are negligible across varying Al thicknesses. This suggests that for these specific systems, other mechanisms dominate over predicted orbital-spin conversion effects in Al.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter magnetism, the precise measurement and null result regarding orbital torques provide critical boundary conditions for theories describing spin-orbit coupling effects in novel material interfaces relevant to quantum spintronics devices.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To experimentally clarify the general effectiveness of orbital contributions (orbital Hall effect, interfacial orbital Rashba effect) to Spin-Orbit Torques (SOTs) in normal metal/ferromagnet heterostructures.

**Main result.** The study found that neither the bulk nor the interface of Aluminum (Al) contributed a detectable torque on adjacent magnetic layers like Co and FePt, suggesting minimal orbital-spin conversion in Al.

**Method.** SOTs were quantified from angle-dependent harmonic Hall voltage measurements ($V_{2\omega}$) performed on fabricated Hall bar devices.

**Model / system.** The research investigates spin-orbit torques in heterostructures involving light metal Aluminum (Al) interfaced with ferromagnetic metals such as Cobalt (Co) and FePt. The analysis contrasts established SHE-driven SOT mechanisms with predicted orbital contributions.

**Key observables.** Dampinglike ($\xi_{	ext{DL}}^E$) and fieldlike ($\xi_{	ext{FL}}^E$) Spin-Orbit Torque efficiencies, measured via $V_{2\omega}$ vs $\phi$ measurements.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Al thickness ($d_{	ext{Al}}$) varying from 0 nm to 4 nm; magnetic materials (Co, FePt).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on fitting the harmonic Hall voltage dependence on the in-plane magnetic field angle ($\phi$) to extract torque components.

**Figures summary.** Figures show $\xi_{	ext{DL}}^E$ and $\xi_{	ext{FL}}^E$ for various bilayers (Pt/Co/Al, FePt/Al, Al/Co) as a function of Al thickness, demonstrating the constancy or near-zero values across different thicknesses.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the scientific problem by contrasting theoretical predictions with experimental measurements. It details the measurement techniques using harmonic Hall voltage analysis on specific bilayers (e.g., $	ext{Pt}/	ext{Co}/	ext{Al}$ and $	ext{FePt}/	ext{Al}$) to quantify SOTs, leading to the conclusion of negligible torque contributions from Al.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The light metal Al was predicted to have strong orbital Hall and Rashba effects from its bulk and interface, respectively. In this letter, we report experimental evidence that neither the bulk nor the interface of the Al contributed a detectable torque on adjacent Co and FePt layers with significant spin Hall effect, spin-orbit coupling, and resistivity mismatch with Al. These results suggest minimal orbital-spin conversion in Al and negligible orbital transport and spin-vorticity torque in Co/Al, Al/Co, and Al/FePt bilayers. Our findings suggest poor generality and/or effectiveness of torque contributions by the orbital Hall effect, the interfacial orbital Rashba effect, and the spin-vorticity coupling.

</details>
